if necessary. Indeed, the subsequences of consecutive values correspond to rows in
\lambda
with equal length (thus when restricting the irrep to
S_{n-1}
, a box can be removed only from the lowest such row), while removing
-(d-1)
corresponds to the possibility that
\lambda
has less than
d
rows.

As an example we now explicitly present the spectrum of
H_{\bigstar_{n}}^{3}.

Example 7.3
.

Let
\lambda=(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3})
be a partition of
n
with three rows. The
d
-QMC irrep Hamiltonian
H_{\bigstar_{n}}^{\lambda}
has at most three distinct eigenvalues, namely

1.

if
\lambda_{1}>\lambda_{2}>\lambda_{3}
then it has three eigenvalues

e_{1}=2(n-\lambda_{3}+2),\quad e_{2}=2(n-\lambda_{2}+1),\quad e_{3}=2(n-\lambda_{1}),

2.

if
\lambda_{1}=\lambda_{2}>\lambda_{3}
then it has two eigenvalues

e_{1}=2(n-\lambda_{3}+2),\quad e_{2}=2(n-\lambda_{2}+1),

3.

if
\lambda_{1}>\lambda_{2}=\lambda_{3}
then it has two eigenvalues

e_{1}=2(n-\lambda_{3}+2),\quad e_{2}=2(n-\lambda_{1}),

4.

if
\lambda_{1}=\lambda_{2}=\lambda_{3}
then it has one eigenvalue

e_{1}=2(n-\lambda_{3}+2).

The solution to the
3
-QMC problem for
{\bigstar_{n}}
is
2(n+1),
attained at partition of the form
\lambda=(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},1)
.

Corollary 7.4
.

Let
n\geq 2
. If
\lambda,\mu
are partitions of
n
with distinct parts, then

\spec(H_{{\bigstar_{n}}}^{\lambda})=\spec(H_{{\bigstar_{n}}}^{\mu})\quad\iff\quad\lambda=\mu.

Remark 7.5
.

The assumption about distinct parts in Corollary
7.4
is necessary. Indeed,
\lambda=(4,2,2,2,2)
and
\mu=(5,5,1,1)
satisfy

\spec(H_{\bigstar_{12}}^{\lambda})=\{16,28\}=\spec(H_{\bigstar_{12}}^{\mu})
.
With a bit more effort, one also can find distinct partitions with equal height such that
H_{{\bigstar_{n}}}^{\lambda}
and
H_{{\bigstar_{n}}}^{\mu}
have the same eigenvalues:

\displaystyle\lambda

\displaystyle=(8,5,5,5,5,2,2),

\displaystyle\mu

\displaystyle=(9,9,4,4,2,2,2)

satisfy
\spec(H_{\bigstar_{32}}^{\lambda})=\{24,31,36\}=\spec(H_{\bigstar_{32}}^{\mu})
.

7.2
Exact solutions for complete bipartite graphs

The star graph
{\bigstar_{n}}
from the previous section is a special example of a complete bipartite graph. We now describe how the solution to the
d
-QMC problem can be obtained for a large class of complete bipartite graphs
K_{n-k,k}
with
k\leq n/2
; by this we mean a graph whose vertices are separated into two subsets
N_{1}
and
N_{2}
of size
n-k
and
k
respectively, each with no internal connections and such that every vertex in
N_{1}
is connected to every vertex in
N_{2}.

The complement of
K_{n-k,k}
consists of two cliques
K_{n-k}
and
K_{k}
(cf. Equation (
7.1
)), hence

K_{n-k,k}=K_{n}-(K_{n-k}\oplus K_{k}).

(7.2)

E.g., if
n=6
and
k=2,
then we have
1
2
3
4
5
6
=
1
2
3
4
5
6
-
\oplus
1
2
3
4
5
6

This gives a formula for the
d
-QMC Hamiltonian
H_{K_{n-k,k}}^{d},

H_{K_{n-k,k}}^{d}=H_{K_{n}}^{d}-\left(H_{K_{n-k}}^{d}{\otimes I_{d^{k}}}+{I_{d^{n-k}}\otimes}H_{K_{k}}^{d}\right).

Note that the summands on the right-hand side commute.
Thus, for
\lambda\vdash n
with at most
d
rows,

H_{K_{n-k,k}}^{\lambda}=H_{K_{n}}^{\lambda}-\left((H_{K_{n-k}}\otimes I)^{\lambda}+(I\otimes H_{K_{k}})^{\lambda}\right).

(7.3)

First, note that
H_{K_{n}}^{\lambda}
is a known scalar matrix (by Lemma
6.4
).
Second, the matrix
H_{K_{n-k}}\otimes I+I\otimes H_{K_{k}}
belongs to the image of the subspace
\mathbb{R}[S_{n-k}]\otimes\id+\id\otimes\mathbb{R}[S_{k}]
under the representation
\rho_{n}^{(d)}
of
S_{n}
.
This subspace is contained in the subalgebra
\mathbb{C}[S_{n-k}]\otimes\mathbb{C}[S_{k}]\cong\mathbb{C}[S_{n-k}\times S_{k}]
of
\mathbb{C}[S_{n}]
.
In order to determine eigenvalues of (
7.3
), it therefore suffices to consider the restriction of the irrep
\lambda
of
S_{n}
to a representation of
S_{n-k}\times S_{k}
.
The matrices
(H_{K_{n-k}}\otimes I)^{\lambda}
and
(I\otimes H_{K_{k}})^{\lambda}
commute, so the eigenvalues of
(H_{K_{n-k}}\otimes I)^{\lambda}+(I\otimes H_{K_{k}})^{\lambda}
are sums of matching eigenvalues of
(H_{K_{n-k}}\otimes I)^{\lambda}
and
(I\otimes H_{K_{k}})^{\lambda}
.
While the latter matrices are similar to
H_{K_{n-k}}^{\lambda}\otimes I
and
I\otimes H_{K_{k}}^{\lambda}
, respectively, the transition matrices involved in similarities are distinct, because projecting to the irrep
\lambda
within
\rho_{n}^{(d)}
does not preserve the tensor decomposition
(\mathbb{C}^{d})^{\otimes(n-k)}\otimes(\mathbb{C}^{d})^{\otimes k}
.
To compute the eigenvalues of (
7.3
), one therefore needs to understand how the restriction of the irrep
\lambda
on
S_{n}
to
S_{n-k}\times S_{k}
decomposes as a direct sum of irreducible representations of
S_{n-k}\times S_{k}
.
Similarly to the case of the star graph, a branching rule is invoked, this time the Littlewood-Richardson rule
[
Sag01
, Section 4.9]
together with the Frobenius reciprocity
[
Sag01
, Theorem 1.12.6]
. More precisely, the restriction of the irreducible module of
S_{n}
corresponding to
\lambda\vdash n
decomposes as

V_{\lambda}^{\downarrow S_{n-k}\times S_{k}}=\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu\vdash n-k,\\
\nu\vdash k\end{subarray}}\big(V_{\mu}\otimes V_{\nu}\big)^{c^{\lambda}_{\mu\nu}},

(7.4)

where
c^{\lambda}_{\mu\nu}
is the Littlewood–Richardson coefficient
[
Sag01
, Section 4.9]
.
Combining (
7.3
) and (
7.4
), the eigenvalues of
H_{K_{n-k,k}}^{\lambda}
are

\Delta(\lambda,\mu,\nu):=\eta_{\lambda}-(\eta_{\mu}+\eta_{\nu})

(7.5)

over all pairs of
\mu\vdash n-k
and
\nu\vdash k
such that the Littlewood–Richardson coefficient
c^{\lambda}_{\mu\nu}
is nonzero.

7.2.1
Littlewood-Richardson coefficients and balanced partitions

In order to explain which triples of partitions
(\lambda,\mu,\nu)
are admissible in (
7.5
), we introduce some further terminology. Let
\mu
be contained in
\lambda
, in the sense that
\mu_{i}\leq\lambda_{i}
for all
i
. A filling
T
of the skew-shaped Young diagram
\lambda/\mu
with natural numbers is a
Littlewood-Richardson (LR) tableau
if

1.

it is a semistandard Young tableau (its entries weakly increase along each row and strictly increase down each column), and

2.

the concatenation of reversed rows in
T
is a lattice word (a word in which every prefix contains at least as many
i
s as
(i+1)
s).

The
content
of a tableau
T
is the partition whose
i
th part counts the number of
i
s in
T
.
By
[
Sag01
, Theorem 4.9.4]
, the Littlewood-Richardson coefficient
c^{\lambda}_{\mu\nu}
counts the number of LR tableaux of shape
\lambda/\mu
with content
\nu
.

Remark 7.6
.

To apply the clique decomposition with more than two summands to the
d
-QMC problem, let

\displaystyle H=H^{d}_{G_{1}}\otimes I_{d^{n-n_{1}}}+I_{d^{n_{1}}}\otimes H^{d}_{G_{2}}\otimes I_{d^{n-n_{1}-n_{2}}}+\cdots+I_{d^{n-n_{r}}}\otimes H^{d}_{G_{r}},

where the graphs
G_{i}
act on pairwise disjoint sets of indices of size
n_{i}
with
n_{1}+\cdots+n_{r}=n.

Then

\displaystyle H^{\lambda}=(H^{d}_{G_{1}}\otimes I_{d^{n-n_{1}}})^{\lambda}+\cdots+(I_{d^{n-n_{r}}}\otimes H^{d}_{G_{r}})^{\lambda}

and the eigenvalues of
H^{\lambda}
are of the form

\alpha_{1}+\cdots+\alpha_{r},

where
\alpha_{i}
are eigenvalues of
H^{\lambda_{i}}_{G_{i}}
and

\lambda_{i}\vdash n_{i}
are such that the
iterated LR coefficient

c^{\lambda}_{\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{r}}
is nonzero. The iterated LR coefficients are inductively defined via the usual LR coefficients
c_{\lambda_{i},\lambda_{i+1}}^{\cdot}
(see
[
KLMS12
,
GL20
]
). In fact,

\displaystyle c^{\lambda}_{\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{r}}=\sum_{\zeta\vdash n-n_{r}}c^{\zeta}_{\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{r-1}}\cdot c^{\lambda}_{\zeta\,\lambda_{r}}=\sum_{\zeta_{1},\ldots,\zeta_{k-2}}c^{\zeta_{1}}_{\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}}c^{\zeta_{2}}_{\zeta_{1}\,\lambda_{3}}\cdots c^{\zeta_{r-2}}_{\zeta_{r-3}\lambda_{r-1}}c^{\lambda}_{\zeta_{k-2}\,\lambda_{r}},

where
\zeta_{i}\vdash n_{1}+\cdots+n_{i}.

The iterated LR coefficients are also invariant under any permutation of the partitions.

In the special case when
G_{i}=K_{n_{i}}
for all
i,

the eigenvalues of
H^{\lambda}
are of the form

\eta_{\lambda_{1}}+\cdots+\eta_{\lambda_{r}},

where
\lambda_{i}\vdash n_{i}
are such that
c^{\lambda}_{\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{r}}
is nonzero.

The above observation may be used to reduce the
d
-QMC problem for a given graph
G
to simpler
d
-QMC problems.
Every graph
G
admits a unique tree clique decomposition
[
BCEHK24
, Theorem 6.3]
as a signed sum of simpler graphs
G_{i}
(e.g., a complete bipartite graph is a clique minus two cliques as in (
7.2
)). The Hamiltonian
H_{G}
then decomposes as a signed sum of Hamiltonians
H_{G_{i}}

[
BCEHK24
, Theorem 6.5]
.
Hence, the iterated LR coefficients dictate as above how eigenvalues of
H_{G}^{\lambda}
express as signed sums of eigenvalues of
H_{G_{i}}^{\lambda_{i}}
.
3
3

3

In
[
BCEHK24
, Section 6.5]
, it is erroneously asserted that eigenvalues of
H_{G}^{\lambda}
are signed Minkowski sums of eigenvalues of
H_{G_{i}}^{\lambda_{i}}
for all
\lambda_{i}
(without the non-vanishing condition on the iterated LR coefficients).

In particular, if
G
is a signed sum of cliques, the iterated LR coefficients and the formula (
6.4
) for
\eta_{\lambda}
give a combinatorial approach to solving the
d
-QMC problem for
G
.

We conjecture that the following is true.

Conjecture 7.7
.

The maximum of
\Delta(\lambda,\mu,\nu)
as in (
7.5
) is attained at a triple of partitions
\lambda\vdash n
,
\mu\vdash n-k
and
\nu\vdash k
such that at most one part of
\lambda
is the sum of a part in
\mu
and a part in
\nu
, while the other parts of
\lambda
are distributed in
\mu
and
\nu
.

Conjecture
7.7
is supported by numerical experiments for all values
n,k,d
with
2k\leq n
,
d<n
,
n\leq 26
.
Proposition
7.8
below establishes a stronger version of Conjecture
7.7
for small
k
or small
d
. In general, Conjecture
7.7
is a first step towards solving the
d
-QMC problem for complete bipartite graphs, as it resolves its combinatorial aspect. Namely, it reduces the
d
-QMC problem to maximizing (
7.5
) over a particular class of triples
(\lambda,\mu,\nu)
for which evidently
c_{\mu\nu}^{\lambda}\neq 0
. One is then left with an integer optimization problem; nevertheless, as Theorem
7.27
below suggests, one may still not expect a straightforward formula for the value of the
d
-QMC problem of a complete bipartite graph.

Given
\mu\vdash n-k
and
\nu\vdash k
, let
\mu\uplus\nu\vdash n
be the partition obtained by merging and sorting the parts of
\mu
and
\nu
(i.e., if partitions are viewed as multisets of rows, then
\mu\uplus\nu
is the disjoint union of
\mu
and
\nu
). We say that
\mu
is a
subpartition
of
\lambda
if
\mu
is obtained from
\lambda
by discarding some rows (this is a stronger condition than
\mu
being contained in
\lambda
). In particular,
\mu
and
\nu
are subpartitions of
\mu\uplus\nu
.

Proposition 7.8
.

When
k\leq 4
or
d\leq 3
, the maximum of
\Delta(\lambda,\mu,\nu)
as in (
7.5
) is attained at a triple of partitions
\mu\vdash n-k
,
\nu\vdash k
and
\lambda=\mu\uplus\nu\vdash n
.
For such a triple, the coefficient
c^{\lambda}_{\mu\nu}
is nonzero.

For the rest of this section we focus on maximizing
\Delta(\mu\uplus\nu,\mu,\nu)
.
The proof of Proposition
7.8
is rather technical, and is presented in Appendix
B
.

Remark 7.9
.

The triple
(n,k,d)=(10,5,5)
is the first where the conclusion of Proposition
7.8
fails.
The solution to the
d
-QMC problem for
K_{5,5}
is
72 attained at

\lambda=(2,2,2,2,2),\;\mu=(2,2,1),\;\nu=(2,2,1).

Maximizing
\Delta(\lambda,\mu,\nu)
over triples
(\lambda,\mu,\nu)
as in Proposition
7.8
yields 70, attained at

\lambda=(3,2,2,2,1),\;\mu=(2,2,1),\;\nu=(3,2)\quad\text{and}\quad\lambda=(3,2,2,2,1),\;\mu=(3,2),\;\nu=({2,2,1}).

Lemma 7.10
.

The expression
\Delta(\mu\uplus\nu,\mu,\nu)
is maximized when
\mu
and
\nu
are balanced.

Proof.

Let
\lambda=\mu\uplus\nu
.
Choose the largest index
k,
for which there is an
l>k
such that

\lambda_{k}=\mu_{i},\lambda_{l}=\mu_{j}
for some
i<j
and

\lambda_{l}-\lambda_{k}\geq 2.
Then construct
\lambda^{\dagger}
(and
\mu^{\dagger}
) by moving a box from
\lambda_{l}
(
\mu_{j}
resp.) to
\lambda_{k}
(
\mu_{i}
resp.). Note that by the choice of
k,
the obtained
\lambda^{\dagger}
and
\mu^{\dagger}
are indeed valid partitions. Then

\displaystyle\eta_{\lambda}-\eta_{\mu}-\eta_{\nu}-(\eta_{\lambda^{\dagger}}-\eta_{\mu^{\dagger}}-\eta_{\nu})=

\displaystyle-(\lambda_{k}-k+1)^{2}+(\lambda_{k}^{\dagger}-k+1)^{2}

\displaystyle-(\lambda_{l}-l+1)^{2}+(\lambda_{l}^{\dagger}-l+1)^{2}

\displaystyle+(\mu_{i}-i+1)^{2}-(\mu_{i}^{\dagger}-i+1)^{2}

\displaystyle+(\mu_{j}-j+1)^{2}-(\mu_{j}^{\dagger}-j+1)^{2}

\displaystyle=\

\displaystyle 2(j-i-l+k)<0

since clearly,
j-i\leq l-k.
After repeating this procedure inductively we deduce that a balanced
\mu
gives the highest value of (
7.5
).
Since the procedure does not affect
\nu
and the rows of
\mu
are still disjoint from the rows of
\nu,
the coefficient
c_{\mu^{\dagger}\nu}^{\lambda}
is nonzero.
By symmetry, the same holds for
\nu
instead of
\mu.

∎

Lemma 7.11
.

The expression
\Delta(\mu\uplus\nu,\mu,\nu)
is maximized when
\het(\mu\uplus\nu)=d
.

Proof.

Let
\lambda=\mu\uplus\nu
.
If
\lambda\vdash n
has height
f<d,
let
l_{0}
be the biggest index such that
\lambda_{l_{0}}>1.

The partition
\lambda^{\dagger}\vdash n
is obtained from
\lambda

by moving the last box of the
l_{0}
th row of
\lambda
to a new row (so that
\lambda^{\dagger}
has
f+1
rows).

Denote
e=\text{ht}(\mu).

If
\lambda_{l_{0}}=\mu_{j}
for some
j,
then
\mu^{\dagger}
is constructed from
\mu
by moving the last box of the
j
th row of
\mu
to a new row (so that
\mu^{\dagger}
has
e+1
rows). Note that by the definition of
l_{0},
we indeed obtain a valid partition. Hence,

\displaystyle\eta_{\lambda}-\eta_{\mu}-(\eta_{\lambda^{\dagger}}-\eta_{\mu^{\dagger}})=\,

\displaystyle\,\frac{f(f-1)(2f-1)}{6}-\frac{(f+1)f(2f+1)}{6}

\displaystyle-\frac{e(e-1)(2e-1)}{6}+\frac{(e+1)e(2e+1)}{6}

\displaystyle-(\lambda_{l_{0}}-l_{0}+1)^{2}+(\lambda^{\dagger}_{l_{0}}-l_{0}+1)^{2}+(\lambda^{\dagger}_{f+1}-f)^{2}

\displaystyle+(\mu_{j}-j+1)^{2}-(\mu^{\dagger}_{j}-j+1)^{2}-(\mu^{\dagger}_{e+1}-e)^{2}

\displaystyle=\,

\displaystyle\,2(e-j-f+l_{0})\leq 0.

If
\lambda_{l_{0}}=\nu_{k}
for some
k,
then construct
\nu^{\dagger}
from
\nu
by moving the last box of the
k
th row of
\nu
to a new row. By analogy with the above computation, we have

\eta_{\lambda}-\eta_{\nu}-(\eta_{\lambda^{\dagger}}-\eta_{\nu^{\dagger}})\leq 0.

It is finally clear that
c_{\mu\nu^{\dagger}}^{\lambda}>0.

∎

By Lemmas
7.10
and
7.11
, we restrict to
\het(\mu\uplus\nu)=d
and balanced
\mu\vdash n-k
,
\nu\vdash k
for the rest of the section.
If
\mu\vdash n-k
and
\nu\vdash k
are such that the last part of
\mu
is not smaller than the first part of
\nu
, we write
\mu\uplus\nu
as
(\mu,\nu)
, to stress that this partition of
n
obtained by concatenating
\mu
and
\nu
. In the following lemmas, whenever
(\mu,\nu)\vdash n
is referred to, it is assumed that
\mu
and
\nu
are suitable for
(\mu,\nu)
to be valid.

Lemma 7.12
.

Suppose
\mu\vdash n-k
and
\nu\vdash k
are such that
(\mu,\nu)
is valid.
Letting
e=\het(\mu)
, we have

\Delta((\mu,\nu),\mu,\nu)=2k(e+n-k).

(7.6)

Proof.

Let
\lambda=(\mu,\nu)
.
Using the formula (
6.4
) from Proposition
6.7
, we have

\begin{split}\Delta(\lambda,\mu,\nu)&=n^{2}-(n-k)^{2}-k^{2}\\
&\phantom{{}={}}+\frac{d(d-1)(2d-1)}{6}-\frac{e(e-1)(2e-1)}{6}-\frac{(d-e)(d-e-1)(2d-2e-1)}{6}\\
&\phantom{{}={}}-\sum_{j=1}^{d}\big(\lambda_{j}-(j-1)\big)^{2}+\sum_{j=1}^{e}\big(\mu_{j}-(j-1)\big)^{2}+\sum_{j=1}^{d-e}\big(\nu_{j}-(j-1)\big)^{2}\end{split}

(7.7)

Since the first
e
rows of
\lambda
form
\mu
,
the third line in (
7.7
) simplifies into

\displaystyle-\sum_{j=e+1}^{d}\big(\lambda_{j}-(j-1)\big)^{2}+\sum_{j=1}^{d-e}\big(\nu_{j}-(j-1)\big)^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\sum_{j=1}^{d-e}\big(\lambda_{j+e}-(j+e-1)\big)^{2}+\sum_{j=1}^{d-e}\big(\nu_{j}-(j-1)\big)^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\sum_{j=1}^{d-e}\big(\nu_{j}-(j+e-1)\big)^{2}+\sum_{j=1}^{d-e}\big(\nu_{j}-(j-1)\big)^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{d-e}e\big(2\nu_{j}-2j-e+2\big)=e(d-d^{2}-e+de+2k).

Putting this back into (
7.7
) and simplifying the obtained expression yields (
7.6
).
∎

Corollary 7.13
.

The solution to the
2
-QMC problem for
K_{n-k,k}
is

2k(1+n-k)
.

Proof.

As explained in the beginning of this section, we are maximizing (
7.6
).
By Proposition
7.8
, this is maximized when

\lambda=(n-k,k)
,
\mu=(n-k)
and
\nu=(k)
. The desired value is then given in Lemma
7.12
.
∎

Lemma 7.14
.

Assume
\mu\vdash n-k
and
\nu\vdash k
are balanced,

\het(\mu)=e
,
and
\lambda=(\mu,\nu)\vdash n
.
Suppose
\mu^{\dagger}
is the balanced partition of
n-k
on
e+1
rows,
\nu^{\dagger}
is the balanced partition of
k
on
d-e-1
rows. If
\lambda^{\dagger}=(\mu^{\dagger},\nu^{\dagger})
is a (valid) partition of
n
, then

\Delta(\lambda^{\dagger},\mu^{\dagger},\nu^{\dagger})>\Delta(\lambda,\mu,\nu).

Proof.

Immediate from Lemma
7.12
.
∎

Lemma 7.15
.

Suppose
\mu\vdash n-k
and
\nu\vdash k
are balanced,

\het(\mu)=e
,
and
\lambda=(\mu,\nu)\vdash n
.
Suppose
\mu^{\dagger}
is the balanced partition of
n-k
on
e+1
rows,
\nu^{\dagger}
is the balanced partition of
k
on
d-e-1
rows. Assume
\lambda^{\dagger}=(\nu^{\dagger},\mu^{\dagger})\vdash n
.
Then

\Delta(\lambda,\mu,\nu)-\Delta(\lambda^{\dagger},\mu^{\dagger},\nu^{\dagger})=2\big((-1+d)k+(1-d+e)n\big).

In particular,

\Delta(\lambda,\mu,\nu)\geq\Delta(\lambda^{\dagger},\mu^{\dagger},\nu^{\dagger})\quad\iff\quad e\geq(d-1)\Big(1-\frac{k}{n}\Big).

Proof.

This is again immediate from Lemma
7.12
.
∎

Further analysis splits into two main cases, according to the following definition.

Definition 7.16
.

We call a triple
(n,k,d)

balancing
if the
rows of the balanced partition of
n
of height
d

can be partitioned into a (balanced) partition of
n-k

and a (balanced) partition of
k
. Otherwise we call the triple

(n,k,d)

unbalancing
.

Remark 7.17
.

Letting
q=\left\lfloor\frac{n}{d}\right\rfloor
and
r=n-qd
, the triple

(n,k,d)
is balancing iff there are integers
0\leq s\leq r
and
0\leq t\leq d-r
such that
k=s(q+1)+tq
. This is equivalent to the existence of an integer
\max\{0,\frac{k-q(d-r)}{q+1}\}\leq s\leq\min\{r,\frac{k}{q+1}\}
such that
k\equiv s\mod q
.

7.2.2
Unbalancing triples

First, we maximize
\Delta(\mu\uplus\nu,\mu,\nu)
when
(n,k,d)
is unbalancing.

Lemma 7.18
.

Let
k,n,d
be positive integers with
k<n
and
n\geq d
. If
\frac{dk}{n}\in\mathbb{N}
, then the rows of the balanced partition
\lambda
of
n
of height
d
can be split into a partition of
k
and a complementary partition of
n-k
.

Proof.

Write

n=qd+r,\qquad 0\leq r<d,

so that the balanced partition
\lambda

consists of
r\times(q+1)
and
(d-r)\times q
.

Set

t=\frac{dk}{n}\in\mathbb{N}.

Because
k<n
, one has
t<d
. Now define

x=\frac{rt}{d},\qquad y=\frac{(d-r)t}{d}.

Then

x=\frac{(n-qd)t}{d}=\frac{nt}{d}-qt=\frac{ndk}{nd}-qt=k-qt,

so
x,y\in\mathbb{N}
. Further,
x+y=t
,
x\leq r
, and
y\leq d-r
. Finally,

\displaystyle x(q+1)+y\,q=q(x+y)+x=qt+\frac{r\,t}{d}=(qd+r)\frac{t}{d}=n\frac{t}{d}=k.

Hence taking
x
of the “large” parts of
\lambda
and
y
of the “small” parts gives a partition of
k
, and the remaining parts sum to
n-k
.
∎

Lemma 7.19
.

Suppose
(n,k,d)
is unbalancing.
Let
e
be the largest integer such that

\lfloor\frac{n-k}{e}\rfloor\geq\lceil\frac{k}{d-e}\rceil
, i.e., the tail of the balanced partition of
n-k
of height
e

is at least as big as the head of the balanced partition of
k
of height
d-e
.

Then

e=\left\lfloor d\Big(1-\frac{k}{n}\Big)\right\rfloor.

(7.8)

Proof.

Since
(n,k,d)
is unbalancing, the head of the balanced partition of
n-k
of height
e+1

is at most as big as the tail of the balanced partition of
k
of height
d-e-1
. This yields the following two inequalities,

\left\lfloor\frac{n-k}{e}\right\rfloor\geq\left\lceil\frac{k}{d-e}\right\rceil,\quad\left\lceil\frac{n-k}{e+1}\right\rceil\leq\left\lfloor\frac{k}{d-e-1}\right\rfloor.

In particular,

\frac{n-k}{e}\geq\frac{k}{d-e},\quad\frac{n-k}{e+1}\leq\frac{k}{d-e-1}.

Clearing denominators yields

d(n-k)-en\geq 0,\quad d(n-k)-(e+1)n\leq 0.

The two inequalities imply

e\leq\frac{d(n-k)}{n},\quad e\geq\frac{d(n-k)}{n}-1.

Since
e
is an integer, this is equivalent to

\left\lceil\frac{d(n-k)}{n}\right\rceil-1\leq e\leq\left\lfloor\frac{d(n-k)}{n}\right\rfloor.

As
(n,k,d)
is unbalancing,
\frac{dk}{n}\not\in\mathbb{N}
by Lemma
7.18
,
whence

\left\lceil\frac{d(n-k)}{n}\right\rceil-1=\left\lfloor\frac{d(n-k)}{n}\right\rfloor,

and (
7.8
) follows.
∎

Lemma 7.20
.

Let
k,n,d
be positive integers with
2k\leq n
and
n\geq d
. Then

\left\lfloor d\Bigl(1-\frac{k}{n}\Bigr)\right\rfloor\;\geq\;(d-1)\Bigl(1-\frac{k}{n}\Bigr)

(7.9)

holds if and only if, letting
dk=r\bmod n
,
one has

r\in\{0\}\;\cup\;\{k,k+1,\dots,n-1\}.

Equivalently, inequality (
7.9
) fails precisely when

1\leq r\leq k-1.

Proof.

If
r=0
, then (
7.9
) holds. Thus assume
r>0
.
Set

A=d\Bigl(1-\frac{k}{n}\Bigr),\quad B=(d-1)\Bigl(1-\frac{k}{n}\Bigr).

(7.10)

Then

\lfloor A\rfloor\geq B\;\iff\;\lfloor A\rfloor+\Bigl(1-\frac{k}{n}\Bigr)\;\geq\;A\;\iff\;\{A\}\;\leq\;1-\frac{k}{n},

where
\{A\}=A-\lfloor A\rfloor
.

From (
7.10
) we have

\{A\}=1-\frac{r}{n}.

Thus

\{A\}\leq 1-\frac{k}{n}\;\iff\;r\geq k,

as claimed.
∎

Corollary 7.21
.

Suppose
(n,k,d)
is unbalancing, and let
dk=r\bmod n
for
0<r<n
.
The maximum of
\Delta(\mu\uplus\nu,\mu,\nu)
equals

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}2k\left(\left\lfloor d\Bigl(1-\frac{k}{n}\Bigr)\right\rfloor+n-k\right)&\text{if }r\geq k\\
2(n-k)\left(d-\left\lfloor d\Bigl(1-\frac{k}{n}\Bigr)\right\rfloor-1+k\right)&\text{if }r<k.\end{array}\right.

Proof.

Let
e^{\prime}=\lfloor d(1-\frac{k}{n})\rfloor
. By Lemma
7.19
,
e^{\prime}
is the largest
e
such that
\lambda=(\mu,\nu)
, with balanced
\mu\vdash n-k
of height
e
and balanced
\nu\vdash k
, is a valid partition of
n
.
If
r\geq k
, then
e^{\prime}\geq(d-1)(1-\frac{k}{n})
by Lemma
7.20
. Thus,
\Delta(\mu\uplus\nu,\mu,\nu)
is maximized at the balanced
\mu\vdash n-k
of height
e^{\prime}
, the balanced
\nu\vdash k
and
\mu\uplus\nu=(\mu,\nu)
by Lemmas
7.14
and
7.15
.
If
0<r<k
, then Lemmas
7.20
and
7.15
show that
\Delta(\mu\uplus\nu,\mu,\nu)
is maximized at the balanced
\mu\vdash n-k
of height
e^{\prime}+1
, the balanced
\nu\vdash k
and
\mu\uplus\nu=(\nu,\mu)
.
In both cases,
the value of
\Delta(\mu\uplus\nu,\mu,\nu)

is then given by Lemma
7.12
.
∎

7.2.3
Balancing triples

Next, we
maximize
\Delta(\mu\uplus\nu,\mu,\nu)
when
(n,k,d)
is balancing.
Throughout this section let
n,k,d\in\mathbb{N}
satisfy

2k\leq n
and
n\geq d
.
Put

q=\Bigl\lfloor\frac{n}{d}\Bigr\rfloor,\qquad r=n-qd\quad(0\leq r<d),

(7.11)

so the balanced partition of weight
n
and height
d
is

\lambda_{\!\hbox to30.04pt{\vbox to5.72pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 15.02132pt\lower-2.8582pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {
{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@stroke@opacity{0.6}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-opacity=0.6} \lxSVG@fill@opacity{0.6}\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill-opacity=0.6} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{}{}{}{}
{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@stroke@opacity{0.6}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-opacity=0.6} \lxSVG@fill@opacity{0.6}\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill-opacity=0.6} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{0.6}{0.0}{0.0}{0.6}{-13.62144pt}{-1.45833pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(0.6 0.0 0.0 0.6 -18.85 -2.02)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}=(\underbrace{q+1,\dots,q+1}_{r\text{ rows}},\;\underbrace{q,\dots,q}_{d-r\text{ rows}}).

Lemma 7.22
.

Suppose
d\mid n
, and let
q=\frac{n}{d}
.
If
q\nmid n-k
, then the triple
(n,k,d)
is unbalanced and thus handled by Corollary
7.21
above.
If
q\mid n-k
and
n-k=eq
, then
the maximum of
\Delta(\mu\uplus\nu,\mu,\nu)
is
attained at
\mu=(q^{e})
,
\nu=(q^{d-e})
and equals

2k(n-k)\Big(1+\frac{d}{n}\Big).

(7.12)

Proof.

Let
\lambda=\mu\uplus\nu
.
Letting
\mu^{+}
denote the balanced partition of
n-k
of height
e+1
, and
\nu^{+}
the balanced partition of
k
of height
d-e-1
, we have
\lambda^{+}=(\nu^{+},\mu^{+})\vdash n
. Thus

\Delta(\lambda^{+},\nu^{+},\mu^{+})=2(n-k)(k+d-e-1),

and using
e=\frac{d(n-k)}{n}
, we get

\Delta(\lambda,\nu,\mu)-\Delta(\lambda^{+},\nu^{+},\mu^{+})=2(n-k)>0.

Increasing
e
further will decrease the height of
\nu^{+}
and thus by Lemma
7.14
only decrease the value of
\Delta
. Thus the maximum
\Delta
is attained at

\mu=(q^{e})
and
\nu=(q^{d-e})
, as claimed. The formula (
7.12
) now follows from Lemma
7.12
.
∎

Proposition 7.23
.

Suppose
\mu\vdash n-k
and
\nu\vdash k
are balanced,
with
\mu_{1}=\nu_{1}
and

\het(\mu)=e
.
Let
s
be such that
\mu_{1}=\cdots=\mu_{s}>\mu_{s+1}=\cdots=\mu_{e}
.
Then

\Delta(\mu\uplus\nu,\mu,\nu)=2(-k^{2}+k(n+s)+(d-e)(e-s)\mu_{e}),

(7.13)

and this function of
e
is maximized at

e^{*}=\frac{d}{2}+\frac{n-2k}{2(q+1)}.

Proof.

Since
\mu,\nu
are balanced and
\mu_{1}=\nu_{1}
, we have

\mu\uplus\nu=(\mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{s},\nu,\mu_{s+1},\ldots,\mu_{e}).

A similar calculation to the one in Lemma
7.12
gives

\Delta(\mu\uplus\nu,\mu,\nu)=2(-k^{2}+k(n+s)+(d-e)\sigma),

(7.14)

where
\sigma=\mu_{s+1}+\cdots+\mu_{e}=(e-s)\mu_{e}
. Note that (
7.14
) becomes (
7.6
) when
s=e
, and hence
\mu\uplus\nu=(\mu,\nu).

Moreover, when
s=0
and
\mu\uplus\nu=(\nu,\mu)
, then
e\mu_{e}=n-k
and hence (
7.14
) becomes
2(n-k)(d-e+k)
, i.e., (
7.6
) with
k
and
e
replaced by
n-k
and
d-e
respectively.

Expressing
s
from

n-k=s(q+1)+(e-s)q,

where
q=\lfloor\frac{n}{d}\rfloor,
and plugging it into (
7.14
) produces a concave quadratic function in
e,
namely

\Delta=2\big(-(q+q^{2})e^{2}+(dq-2kq+nq+dq^{2})e-2k^{2}+2kn+dkq-dnq\big).

(7.15)

This function attains its maximum at

e^{*}=\frac{d}{2}+\frac{n-2k}{2(q+1)}.\qed

(7.16)

Remark 7.24
.

Using the obvious inequalities

\frac{n}{d}-1\leq\left\lfloor\frac{n}{d}\right\rfloor\leq\frac{n}{d},

we obtain the following bounds on
e^{*}
:

\frac{d}{2}+\frac{d(n-2k)}{2(d+n)}\leq e^{*}\leq d\Big(1-\frac{k}{n}\Big).

Assume
(n,k,d)
is balancing, and w.l.o.g.,
n-k\geq k
.
The strategy of the proof is as follows:
by Lemmas
7.10
and
7.11
, we know that
\Delta(\mu\uplus\nu,\mu,\nu)
in
(
7.5
) is maximized with
\het(\mu)=\het(\nu)=d
and

\mu\vdash n-k
,
\nu\vdash k
being balanced.
Such pairs are thus uniquely determined by
e=\het(\mu)
.
Note that
\mu\uplus\nu=(\mu,\nu)
for small
e
, and
\mu\uplus\nu=(\nu,\mu)
for large
e
.
By Lemma
7.14
,
\Delta
increases while
\mu
forms the top rows of
\mu\uplus\nu
and
e
increases. Conversely,
\Delta
decreases when
\nu
is the top of
\mu\uplus\nu
and
e
increases.
It is thus key to analyze transitions, where the rows of
\mu
,
\nu
appear mixed in
\mu\uplus\nu
.

For ease of notation,
let
\mu[e]
denote the balanced partition of
n-k

with height
e
. Similarly,

\nu[e]
denotes the balanced partition of
k
of height
d-e
.
Let

e_{0}=\max\left\{e\in\{1,\ldots,d-1\}\colon\left\lfloor\frac{n-k}{e}\right\rfloor\geq\bigg\lceil\frac{k}{d-e}\bigg\rceil\right\}.

(7.17)

Since
n-k\geq k
,
e=1
belongs to the right-hand side set.
Similarly, let

e_{1}=\min\left\{e\in\{1,\ldots,d-1\}\colon\left\lfloor\frac{k}{d-e}\right\rfloor\geq\bigg\lceil\frac{n-k}{e}\bigg\rceil\right\}.

(7.18)

If the set in the definition of
e_{1}
is empty
(e.g.,
n=5
,
k=2
,
d=2
), we set
e_{1}=d-1
.
By Lemma
7.14
, the
e
maximizing the
d
-QMC Hamiltonian for
K_{n-k,k}
will satisfy
e_{0}\leq e\leq e_{1}
.

Let
\lambda_{\!\hbox to30.04pt{\vbox to5.72pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 15.02132pt\lower-2.8582pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {

with height

Set

\lxSVG@closescope }{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{0.6}{0.0}{0.0}{0.6}{-13.62144pt}{-1.45833pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(0.6 0.0 0.0 0.6 -18.85 -2.02)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

Since

is balancing,

.
Further,

, or

and

.

}
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}
.

The partition
\mu
is of the form, say,
((q+1)^{x},q^{y})
for some
x,y\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
. Thus

\displaystyle x+y

\displaystyle=e,

(7.19)

\displaystyle x(q+1)+yq

\displaystyle=n-k,

\displaystyle 0\leq x\leq r

\displaystyle\text{and}\quad 0\leq y\leq d-r.

Substituting
y=e-x
into the second equation of (
7.19
) gives

\displaystyle x+eq

\displaystyle=n-k.

(7.20)

Likewise,
if
\mu^{\prime}=((q+1)^{x^{\prime}},q^{y^{\prime}})
for some
x^{\prime},y^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, then

\displaystyle x^{\prime}+e^{\prime}q

\displaystyle=n-k.

(7.21)

Equating (
7.20
) and (
7.21
), we have

\displaystyle x-x^{\prime}

\displaystyle=q(e^{\prime}-e).

(7.22)

Since
e<e^{\prime}
,
x>x^{\prime}
.
Further, by considering the difference in the number of
q
-sized parts:

\displaystyle y^{\prime}-y

\displaystyle=(e^{\prime}-x^{\prime})-(e-x)=(e^{\prime}-e)+(x-x^{\prime})

(7.23)

\displaystyle=(e^{\prime}-e)+q(e^{\prime}-e)=(1+q)(e^{\prime}-e),

whence
y^{\prime}>y
.

Now, let
e^{\prime\prime}
be an integer such that
e<e^{\prime\prime}<e^{\prime}
. Define
\delta=e^{\prime\prime}-e
. Then
0<\Delta e<e^{\prime}-e
.
We propose constructing
\mu^{\prime\prime}
composed of
x^{\prime\prime}
parts of size
q+1
and
y^{\prime\prime}
parts of size
q
, defined as follows:

\displaystyle x^{\prime\prime}

\displaystyle=x-q\delta

(7.24)

\displaystyle y^{\prime\prime}

\displaystyle=y+(1+q)\delta

We shall verify that
\mu^{\prime\prime}
satisfies the required conditions.

Firstly, the height of
\mu
is

\displaystyle\het(\mu^{\prime\prime})

\displaystyle=x^{\prime\prime}+y^{\prime\prime}=(x-q\delta)+(y+(1+q)\delta)=(x+y)+\delta=e+(e^{\prime\prime}-e)=e^{\prime\prime}.

Next,
\mu^{\prime\prime}\vdash n-k
, as

\displaystyle x^{\prime\prime}(q+1)+y^{\prime\prime}q

\displaystyle=\big(x-q\delta\big)(q+1)+\big(y+(1+q)\delta\big)q

\displaystyle=\big(x(q+1)+yq\big)-(q^{2}+q)\delta+(q+q^{2})\delta=x(q+1)+yq=n-k.

Finally, we need to show
0\leq x^{\prime\prime}\leq r
and
0\leq y^{\prime\prime}\leq d-r
. Clearly,

x^{\prime\prime}=x-q\delta<x\leq r
.
Also,
x^{\prime\prime}=x-q\delta\geq x-q(e^{\prime}-e)=x^{\prime}\geq 0
.
To verify the desired properties of
y^{\prime\prime}
, we have

y^{\prime\prime}=y+(1+q)\delta>y\geq 0
, and

y^{\prime\prime}=y+(1+q)\delta e<y+(1+q)(e^{\prime}-e)=y^{\prime}\leq d-r
. This completes the proof.
∎

By definition of
e_{0}
and
e_{1},
whenever
e\leq e_{0},
the partition
\lambda\vdash n
made of rows of
\mu[e],\nu[e]
is
(\mu[e],\nu[e])
. If
e\in\mathfrak{E},
then this partition
\lambda
equals
\lambda_{\!\hbox to30.04pt{\vbox to5.72pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 15.02132pt\lower-2.8582pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {
{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@stroke@opacity{0.6}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-opacity=0.6} \lxSVG@fill@opacity{0.6}\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill-opacity=0.6} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{}{}{}{}
{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@stroke@opacity{0.6}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-opacity=0.6} \lxSVG@fill@opacity{0.6}\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill-opacity=0.6} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{0.6}{0.0}{0.0}{0.6}{-13.62144pt}{-1.45833pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(0.6 0.0 0.0 0.6 -18.85 -2.02)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}
, and is of the form

\lambda=(\mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{s},\nu,\mu_{s+1},\ldots,\mu_{e}),

where
\mu_{1}=\cdots=\mu_{s}>\mu_{s+1}=\cdots=\mu_{e}

(note the two edge cases,
s=0
or
s=e
can also both occur).
If
e\geq e_{1},
then
\lambda
is of the form
(\nu[e],\mu[e]).
Note that
(\mu[e_{0}],\nu[e_{0}])

may or may not be balanced, and the same holds for
(\nu[e_{1}],\mu[e_{1}]).

Lemma 7.26
.

If
e_{0}\not\in\mathfrak{E}
, then
e_{0}+1\in\mathfrak{E}
.
Likewise, if
e_{1}\not\in\mathfrak{E}
, then
e_{1}-1\in\mathfrak{E}
. In particular,
\mathfrak{E}
is one of the following four discrete intervals:

\{e_{0},e_{0}+1,\ldots,e_{1}\},\;\{e_{0},e_{0}+1,\ldots,e_{1}-1\},\;\{e_{0}+1,e_{0}+2,\ldots,e_{1}\},\;\{e_{0}+1,e_{0}+2,\ldots,e_{1}-1\}.

Proof.

Suppose
e_{0}\not\in\mathfrak{E}
, and consider
e_{0}+1
. By definition of
e_{0},

\left\lfloor\frac{n-k}{e_{0}+1}\right\rfloor<\bigg\lceil\frac{k}{d-e_{0}-1}\bigg\rceil.

Since
\min\mathfrak{E}\geq e_{0}+1
, we deduce

\left\lfloor\frac{n-k}{\min\mathfrak{E}}\right\rfloor\leq\left\lfloor\frac{n-k}{e_{0}+1}\right\rfloor<\bigg\lceil\frac{k}{d-e_{0}-1}\bigg\rceil\leq\bigg\lceil\frac{k}{d-\min\mathfrak{E}}\bigg\rceil.

(7.25)

By definition of
\mathfrak{E}
, the partitions

\mu[\min\mathfrak{E}]
,
\nu[\min\mathfrak{E}]
combine to form
\lambda_{\!\hbox to30.04pt{\vbox to5.72pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 15.02132pt\lower-2.8582pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {
{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@stroke@opacity{0.6}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-opacity=0.6} \lxSVG@fill@opacity{0.6}\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill-opacity=0.6} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{}{}{}{}
{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@stroke@opacity{0.6}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-opacity=0.6} \lxSVG@fill@opacity{0.6}\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill-opacity=0.6} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{0.6}{0.0}{0.0}{0.6}{-13.62144pt}{-1.45833pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(0.6 0.0 0.0 0.6 -18.85 -2.02)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}
,
whence

\bigg\lceil\frac{k}{d-\min\mathfrak{E}}\bigg\rceil-\left\lfloor\frac{n-k}{\min\mathfrak{E}}\right\rfloor\leq 1.

Hence the same holds with
\min\mathfrak{E}
replaced by
e_{0}+1
by
(
7.25
). Thus
\mu[e_{0}+1]
,
\nu[e_{0}+1]
must combine to form
\lambda_{\!\hbox to30.04pt{\vbox to5.72pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 15.02132pt\lower-2.8582pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {
{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@stroke@opacity{0.6}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-opacity=0.6} \lxSVG@fill@opacity{0.6}\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill-opacity=0.6} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{}{}{}{}
{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@stroke@opacity{0.6}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-opacity=0.6} \lxSVG@fill@opacity{0.6}\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill-opacity=0.6} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{0.6}{0.0}{0.0}{0.6}{-13.62144pt}{-1.45833pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(0.6 0.0 0.0 0.6 -18.85 -2.02)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}
, too. That is,
e_{0}+1\in\mathfrak{E}
.

The proof for
e_{1}
is the same.
∎

7.2.4
Summary

We combine the preceding results on unbalancing and balancing triples into the following statement.

Theorem 7.27
.

Let
n\geq 2k
and
n>d
.
Let
e_{\star}
be the closest integer in

\mathfrak{E}
to
e^{*}
. Then the maximum of
\Delta(\mu\uplus\nu)=\eta_{\mu\uplus\nu}-\eta_{\mu}-\eta_{\nu}
for
\mu\vdash n-k
and
\nu\vdash k
is attained at one of the following pairs:

1.

\mu
is balanced of height
e_{0}
and
\nu
is balanced of height
d-e_{0}
;

2.

\mu
is balanced of height
e_{\star}
and
\nu
is balanced of height
d-e_{\star}
;

3.

\mu
is balanced of height
e_{1}
and
\nu
is balanced of height
d-e_{1}
.

Proof.

If the triple
(n,k,d)
is unbalancing, then
\mathfrak{E}=\emptyset
, and the maximum is attained at (a) or (c) by Corollary
7.21
.
Now suppose that the triple
(n,k,d)
is balancing.
By Lemma
7.14
, the maximum of
\Delta(\mu\uplus\nu,\mu,\nu)
is attained for a balanced
\mu\vdash n-k
of height
e
and a balanced
\nu\vdash k
of height
d-e
, where
e
is an integer between
e_{0}
and
e_{1}
.
The characterization of
\mathfrak{E}
as in Lemma
7.26
and maximization of the function (
7.13
) at
e^{*}
in Proposition
7.23
show that the maximum is attained for
e\in\{e_{0},e_{1},e_{\star}\}
, which gives rise to the cases (a), (b) and (c).
∎

Proposition
7.8
and Theorem
7.27
resolve the
d
-QMC problem for
K_{n-k,k}
when either
k
or
d
is small.

Corollary 7.28
.

Let
k\leq 4
and
d<n
.
Let
e_{\star}
be the closest integer in

\mathfrak{E}
to
e^{*}
. Then the solution to the
d
-QMC problem for
K_{n-k,k}
is the biggest of the following three values

\eta_{\mu\uplus\nu}-\eta_{\mu}-\eta_{\nu}

for
\mu\vdash n-k
and
\nu\vdash k
, where either

1.

\mu
is balanced of height
e_{0}
and
\nu
is balanced of height
d-e_{0}
;

2.

\mu
is balanced of height
e_{\star}
and
\nu
is balanced of height
d-e_{\star}
;

3.

\mu
is balanced of height
e_{1}
and
\nu
is balanced of height
d-e_{1}
.

Corollary 7.29
.

The solution to the 3-QMC problem for
K_{n-k,k}
is

\begin{cases}2(k+1)(n-k)&\text{if }n<3k\\
2k(n-k+2)&\text{if }n\geq 3k\end{cases}.

Proof.

Observe that
e_{1}=2
for all
n\geq 2k
, and that

e_{0}=2
iff
n\geq 3k
; otherwise
e_{0}=1
.
Thus if
n\geq 3k
we are in case (c) of Theorem
7.27
.

By Theorem
7.27
, there are only two cases to consider:

\lambda=\mu\uplus\nu
with

\mu=(n-k),\;\nu=\left(\left\lceil\frac{k}{2}\right\rceil,\left\lfloor\frac{k}{2}\right\rfloor\right)\quad\text{or}\quad\mu=\left(\left\lceil\frac{n-k}{2}\right\rceil,\left\lfloor\frac{n-k}{2}\right\rfloor\right),\;\nu=(k).

In the first case
\lambda=(\mu,\nu)
and thus

\begin{split}\eta_{\mu\uplus\nu}-\eta_{\mu}-\eta_{\nu}&=-2(k-n-1)\left(\left\lceil\frac{k}{2}\right\rceil+\left\lfloor\frac{k}{2}\right\rfloor\right)\\
&=2k(n-k+1).\end{split}

(7.26)

Now assume
n<3k
, and consider the second case. Then

\mu\uplus\nu=\left(k,\left\lceil\frac{n-k}{2}\right\rceil,\left\lfloor\frac{n-k}{2}\right\rfloor\right)
,
so

\begin{split}\eta_{\mu\uplus\nu}-\eta_{\mu}-\eta_{\nu}&=-\left(\left\lceil\frac{n-k}{2}\right\rceil+\left\lfloor\frac{n-k}{2}\right\rfloor\right)^{2}+\left(\left\lceil\frac{n-k}{2}\right\rceil+\left\lfloor\frac{n-k}{2}\right\rfloor+k\right)^{2}+\left\lceil\frac{n-k}{2}\right\rceil^{2}\\
&\phantom{{}={}}-\left(\left\lceil\frac{n-k}{2}\right\rceil-1\right)^{2}-\left(\left\lfloor\frac{n-k}{2}\right\rfloor-2\right)^{2}+\left(\left\lfloor\frac{n-k}{2}\right\rfloor-1\right)^{2}-k^{2}+4\\
&=-\left(-\left\lfloor\frac{n-k}{2}\right\rfloor-k+n-1\right)^{2}+\left(-\left\lfloor\frac{n-k}{2}\right\rfloor-k+n\right)^{2}\\
&\phantom{{}={}}-\left(\left\lfloor\frac{n-k}{2}\right\rfloor-2\right)^{2}+\left(\left\lfloor\frac{n-k}{2}\right\rfloor-1\right)^{2}-k^{2}-(n-k)^{2}+n^{2}+4\\
&=2(k+1)(n-k)\end{split}

(7.27)

The difference between (
7.27
) and (
7.26
) is

2k(-1+k-n)-2(1+k)(k-n)=2n-4k\geq 0

since
n\geq 2k
. In particular, for
d=3
we are always in case (c) of Theorem
7.27
.

If
n\geq 3k
, then

\mu\uplus\nu=\left(\left\lceil\frac{n-k}{2}\right\rceil,\left\lfloor\frac{n-k}{2}\right\rfloor,k\right)
, and
a similar calculation to the above shows that

\eta_{\mu\uplus\nu}-\eta_{\mu}-\eta_{\nu}=2k(n-k+2),

(7.28)

concluding the proof.
∎

\begin{array}[]{c|c|c|c|c|c}(n,k,d)&e_{0}&e_{1}&e^{*}&e_{\rm max}&\mathfrak{E}\\[2.84526pt]
\hline\cr(4,2,3)&1&2&\frac{3}{2}&{1,2}&\{1,2\}\\[2.84526pt]
(5,2,3)&1&2&\frac{7}{4}&{2}&\{2\}\\[2.84526pt]
(5,2,4)&2&3&\frac{9}{4}&{2}&\{2,3\}\\[2.84526pt]
(8,2,3)&2&2&\frac{13}{6}&{2}&\{2\}\\[2.84526pt]
(9,2,8)&6&7&\frac{21}{4}&{6}&\{6,7\}\\[2.84526pt]
(6,3,4)&1&3&2&{2}&\{2\}\\[2.84526pt]
(7,3,4)&2&3&\frac{9}{4}&{2}&\{2\}\\[2.84526pt]
(11,3,5)&3&4&\frac{10}{3}&{3}&\{4\}\\[2.84526pt]
\end{array}

\begin{array}[]{c|c|c|c|c|c}(n,k,d)&e_{0}&e_{1}&e^{*}&e_{\rm max}&\mathfrak{E}\\[2.84526pt]
\hline\cr(8,4,2)&1&1&1&{1}&\{1\}\\[2.84526pt]
(8,4,3)&1&2&\frac{3}{2}&{1,2}&\{\}\\[2.84526pt]
(8,4,6)&2&4&3&{3}&\{2,3,4\}\\[2.84526pt]
(9,4,3)&1&2&\frac{13}{8}&{2}&\{\}\\[2.84526pt]
(9,4,4)&2&3&\frac{13}{6}&{2}&\{2\}\\[2.84526pt]
(9,4,5)&2&3&\frac{11}{4}&{3}&\{3\}\\[2.84526pt]
(11,4,4)&2&3&\frac{5}{2}&{2}&\{\}\\[2.84526pt]
\end{array}

Table 1:
A selection of triples and the values of
e_{0}
,
e_{1}
,
e^{*}
,
e_{\rm max}
, and
\mathfrak{E}
for these triples. The triple
(11,3,5)
is an example where the optimal
\lambda=\mu\uplus\nu
is not balanced. The triples on the right side demonstrate that any assortment of cases (a),(b),(c) in Corollary
7.28
can give rise to the maximum value.

8
Separation of irreps in
d
-QMC

Given
n,d\in\mathbb{N}
and a partition
\lambda\vdash n
with at most
d
rows, we are interested in adapting the NPO hierarchy in Section
5
to compute the largest eigenvalue of
H_{G}^{\lambda}
for a general weighted graph
G
on
n
vertices.
To isolate the irreducible representation
\rho_{\lambda}
of
\mathcal{A}^{\text{Sw}_{d}}_{n}=\mathcal{F}_{n}/\mathcal{I}^{\text{Sw}_{d}}_{n}
corresponding to the partition
\lambda
of
n
, one needs to adjoin to
\mathcal{I}^{\text{Sw}_{d}}_{n}
some polynomials in
\mathcal{F}_{n}
that vanish in
\rho_{\lambda}(\mathbb{C}S_{n})
, but not in
\rho_{\mu}(\mathbb{C}S_{n})
for any
\mu\neq\lambda
.

In general it suffices a add a single polynomial, chosen as follows.
Given a partition
\lambda\vdash n
let
s_{\lambda}\in\mathbb{C}S_{n}
be a Young symmetrizer corresponding to
\lambda

[
Pro07
, Section 9.2.2]
. Then
\frac{\dim\rho_{\lambda}}{n!}s_{\lambda}
is a primitive idempotent in
\mathbb{C}S_{n}
that generates
\rho_{n}
as a left ideal
[
Pro07
, Theorems 9.2.4.1 and 9.2.4.2]
. Hence

\hat{s}_{\lambda}=\frac{\dim\rho_{\lambda}}{(n!)^{2}}\sum_{\sigma\in S_{n}}\sigma s_{\lambda}\sigma^{-1}

is a centrally primitive idempotent in
\mathbb{C}S_{n}
, generating
\rho_{\lambda}
as a two-sided ideal.

Proposition 8.1
.

Let
\lambda\vdash n
.
Then
\rho_{\lambda}(\id-\hat{s}_{\lambda})=0
and
\rho_{\mu}(\id-\hat{s}_{\lambda})\neq 0
every partition
\mu\neq\lambda
.

Proof.

The centrally primitive idempotents
\{\hat{s}_{\lambda}\}_{\lambda\vdash n}
are pairwise orthogonal, so
(\id-\hat{s}_{\lambda})\hat{s}_{\mu}=0
if
\lambda=\mu
and
\hat{s}_{\mu}
otherwise.
Therefore
\rho_{\lambda}(\id-\hat{s}_{\lambda})=0
and
\rho_{\mu}(\id-\hat{s}_{\lambda})\neq 0
for
\mu\neq\lambda
.
∎

Lifting
\hat{s}_{\lambda}
to an element of
\mathcal{F}_{n}
yields the desired polynomial. However, this polynomial has high degree (not much smaller than
n
). On the other hand, the symmetric group relations (
1.4
) have degree at most
3
, and the degree-reducing antisymmetrizer relation (
3.1
) has degree
d
. Therefore the above approach is not appealing from a computational perspective.
Instead, it is preferable to find low-degree polynomials that distinguish
\lambda
from other partitions of
n
with at most
d
rows.

In
[
BCEHK24
]
it was shown that for
d=2
, the value
\eta_{\lambda}
from Example
6.6
separates irreps with at most two rows.
Therefore,
\eta_{\lambda}-h_{K_{n}}
is a linear polynomial that separates irreps of
\mathcal{A}^{\text{Sw}_{2}}_{n}
.
In particular, the largest eigenvalue of
H_{G}^{\lambda}
for a two-row partition
\lambda\vdash n
equals the NPO problem

\min\left\{\alpha:\alpha-h_{G}=\sum_{k}s_{k}^{*}s_{k}+q\text{ for some }s_{k}\in\mathcal{F}_{n},\ q\in\mathcal{I}^{\text{Sw}_{d}}_{n}+\big(\eta_{\lambda}-h_{K_{n}}\big)\right\},

and can thus be handled using standard SDP-based NPO hierarchies.

The same does not apply when
d=3
, as
\eta_{\lambda}
in (
6.4
) does not separate irreps with at most three rows. For example, partitions
\lambda=(4,1,1)
and
\mu=(3,3)
of
n=6
give
\eta_{\lambda}=\eta_{\mu}=24
.
Even more,
\eta_{\lambda}
does not separate irreps with three rows; e.g.,
\lambda=(5,2,2)
and
\mu=(4,4,1)
give
\eta_{\lambda}=\eta_{\mu}=60
.
Below, we present a method of separating irreps with at most three rows in the spirit of
3
-QMC, and a method of separating general irreps that is suitable for solving the localized
d
-QMC problem of finding the largest eigenvalue of
H_{G}^{\lambda}
.

8.1
Separation of irreps with at most three rows via two graphs

First we show that the spectra of the Hamiltonians corresponding to the clique
K_{n}
and the star graph
{\bigstar_{n}}
(which were analyzed in Subsections
6.2
and
7.1
, respectively) distinguish partitions with at most three rows.

Proposition 8.2
.

Let
n\geq 2
. The following are equivalent for partitions
\lambda,\mu\vdash n
with at most three rows:

1.

\spec(H_{K_{n}}^{\lambda})=\spec(H_{K_{n}}^{\mu})
and
\spec(H_{{\bigstar_{n}}}^{\lambda})\subseteq\spec(H_{{\bigstar_{n}}}^{\mu})
;

2.

\spec(H_{{\bigstar_{n}}}^{\lambda})=\spec(H_{{\bigstar_{n}}}^{\mu})
;

3.

{\lambda}={\mu}
.

Proof.

It is clear that (iii) implies both (i) and (ii).

(i)
\Rightarrow
(iii): By Example
7.1
and Example
7.3
,
the eigenvalues of
nI-\frac{1}{2}H_{{\bigstar_{n}}}^{\lambda}
are obtained from the sequence

{\lambda}_{1}>{\lambda}_{2}-1>{\lambda}_{3}-2
by keeping only the smallest element of any subsequence of consecutive values, and then removing
-2
if necessary.
Consequently,

{\lambda}_{1}+({\lambda}_{2}-1)+({\lambda}_{3}-2)=n-3={\mu}_{1}+({\mu}_{2}-1)+({\mu}_{3}-2)

(8.1)

and

\spec(H_{{\bigstar_{n}}}^{\lambda})\subseteq\spec(H_{{\bigstar_{n}}}^{\mu})

immediately imply
\lambda=\mu
if
|\spec(H_{{\bigstar_{n}}}^{\lambda})|\geq 2
.
Now assume
|\spec(H_{{\bigstar_{n}}}^{\lambda})|=1
. By Proposition
6.7
,
\spec(H_{K_{n}}^{\lambda})=\spec(H_{K_{n}}^{\mu})
implies

{\lambda}_{1}^{2}+({\lambda}_{2}-1)^{2}+({\lambda}_{3}-2)^{2}={\mu}_{1}^{2}+({\mu}_{2}-1)^{2}+({\mu}_{3}-2)^{2}

(8.2)

We distinguish three cases:

1.

{\lambda}=(\frac{n}{3},\frac{n}{3},\frac{n}{3})
, and the sole eigenvalue of
nI-\frac{1}{2}H_{{\bigstar_{n}}}^{\lambda}
is
\frac{n}{3}-2
. Since
{\mu}_{1}\geq\frac{n}{3}
, if follows that
{\mu}_{2}-1=\frac{n}{3}-2
or
{\mu}_{3}-2=\frac{n}{3}-2
.
In the latter case
\mu=\lambda
, so let us suppose the former holds. Then (
8.1
) and (
8.2
) imply

\displaystyle n-3

\displaystyle={\mu}_{1}+(\tfrac{n}{3}-2)+({\mu}_{3}-2),

\displaystyle(\tfrac{n}{3})^{2}+(\tfrac{n}{3}-1)^{2}+(\tfrac{n}{3}-2)^{2}

\displaystyle={\mu}_{1}^{2}+(\tfrac{n}{3}-2)^{2}+({\mu}_{3}-2)^{2}.

Expressing
{\mu}_{3}=\frac{2n}{3}+1-{\mu}_{1}
gives

(\tfrac{n}{3})^{2}+(\tfrac{n}{3}-1)^{2}={\mu}_{1}^{2}+(\tfrac{2n}{3}-{\mu}_{1}-1)^{2},

which has solutions
{\mu}_{1}=\frac{n}{3}
and
{\mu}_{1}=\frac{n}{3}-1
. The first one implies
{\mu}={\lambda}
, while the second one contradicts the fact that
{\mu}
is a partition of
n
.

2.

{\lambda}=(\frac{n}{2},\frac{n}{2})
, and the sole eigenvalue of
nI-\frac{1}{2}H_{{\bigstar_{n}}}^{\lambda}
is
\frac{n}{2}-1
. Since
{\mu}_{3}<\frac{n}{2}
, it follows that
{\mu}_{1}=\frac{n}{2}-1
or
{\mu}_{2}-1=\frac{n}{2}-1
.
In the latter case
{\mu}={\lambda}
, so let us assume the former holds.
Then (
8.1
) and (
8.2
) imply

\displaystyle n-3

\displaystyle=(\tfrac{n}{2}-1)+({\mu}_{2}-1)+({\mu}_{3}-2),

\displaystyle(\tfrac{n}{2})^{2}+(\tfrac{n}{2}-1)^{2}+(0-2)^{2}

\displaystyle=(\tfrac{n}{2}-1)^{2}+({\mu}_{2}-1)^{2}+({\mu}_{3}-2)^{2}.

Expressing
{\mu}_{2}=\tfrac{n}{2}+1-{\mu}_{3}
gives

(\tfrac{n}{2})^{2}+4=(\tfrac{n}{2}-{\mu}_{3})^{2}+({\mu}_{3}-2)^{2},

which has solutions
{\mu}_{3}=0
and
{\mu}_{3}=\frac{n}{2}+2
. The first one implies
{\mu}={\lambda}
, while the second one contradicts the fact that
{\mu}
is a partition of
n
.

3.

{\lambda}=(n)
, and the sole eigenvalue of
nI-\frac{1}{2}H_{{\bigstar_{n}}}^{\lambda}
is
n
. Then
{\mu}_{3}\leq{\mu}_{2}\leq n
implies
{\mu}_{1}=n
, and so
{\mu}={\lambda}
.

(ii)
\Rightarrow
(iii): As in the previous paragraph we see that (ii) and (
8.1
) imply
{\lambda}={\mu}
if
|\spec(H_{{\bigstar_{n}}}^{\lambda})|\geq 2
.
On the other hand, if
|\spec(H_{{\bigstar_{n}}}^{\lambda})|=|\spec(H_{{\bigstar_{n}}}^{\mu})|=1
then
{\lambda},{\mu}\in\{(n),(\frac{n}{2},\frac{n}{2}),(\frac{n}{3},\frac{n}{3},\frac{n}{3})\}
. For these three cases, the star graph Hamiltonian has eigenvalue
0
,
n+2
or
\frac{4}{3}n+4
. Thus
\spec(H_{{\bigstar_{n}}}^{\lambda})=\spec(H_{{\bigstar_{n}}}^{\mu})
only if
{\lambda}={\mu}
.
∎

Remark 8.3
.

Let
n=9
. For
{\lambda}=(3,3,3)
and
{\mu}=(6,2,1)
we have

\spec(H_{{\bigstar_{n}}}^{\lambda})=\{16\}\subseteq\{20,16,6\}=\spec(H_{{\bigstar_{n}}}^{\mu}).

Therefore the role of
K_{n}
in Proposition
8.2
(i) is essential (note that
\eta_{\lambda}=72
and
\eta_{\mu}=48
).
Likewise, the restriction to partitions with at most three rows is required (cf. Remark
7.5
).
Namely, let
n=21
,
{\lambda}=(7,7,7)
and
\mu=(9,6,5,1)
. Then
\eta_{\lambda}=336=\eta_{\mu}
and

\spec(H_{{\bigstar_{n}}}^{\lambda})=\{16\}\subseteq\{12,16,18,23\}=\spec(H_{{\bigstar_{n}}}^{\mu}).

Let
\lambda\vdash n
be a three-row partition, and let
m
be the minimal polynomial of
H_{{\bigstar_{n}}}^{\lambda}
; note that
m
is of degree at most 3, and determined by Example
7.3
.
As a consequence of Proposition
8.2
, the largest eigenvalue of
H_{G}^{\lambda}
for
d=3
is the solution of the NPO problem

\min\left\{\alpha:\alpha-h_{G}=\sum_{k}s_{k}^{*}s_{k}+q\text{ for some }s_{k}\in\mathcal{F}_{n},\ q\in\mathcal{I}^{\text{Sw}_{3}}_{n}+\big(\eta_{\lambda}-h_{K_{n}},m(h_{{\bigstar_{n}}})\big)\right\}.

8.2
Separation of irreps via low-degree central elements

In this section we show that partitions
\lambda\vdash n
with at most
d
rows can be distinguished by
d
relations of degrees
1,\dots,d
, which can be used in an NPO problem for solving the localized
d
-QMC problem, i.e., finding the largest eigenvalue of
H_{G}^{\lambda}
.

For
2\leq k\leq n
let
q_{k}\in\mathbb{C}[S_{n}]
be the sum of all
k
-cycles in
S_{n}
(there are
(k-1)!\binom{n}{k}
of them).
Since
q_{k}
is central in
\mathbb{C}[S_{n}]
, we have

\rho_{\lambda}(q_{k})=\gamma_{k,\lambda}I
,
where

\gamma_{k,\lambda}\,\chi_{\lambda}(e)=\text{Tr}\big(\rho_{\lambda}(q_{k})\big)=(k-1)!\binom{n}{k}\,\chi_{\lambda}((1\,\dots\,k)).

(8.3)

Note that

\gamma_{2,\lambda}=\eta_{\lambda}=n^{2}+\frac{d(d-1)(2d-1)}{6}-\sum_{k=1}^{d}\big(\lambda_{k}-(k-1)\big)^{2}

by Proposition
6.7
.
The other values
\gamma_{k,\lambda}
can be computed using the normalized character formula
[
Lsa08
, Theorem 4]
(with the Murnaghan-Nakayama rule, cf. Appendix
D.1
, at its core), and are in particular integers.
For example,

\displaystyle\gamma_{3,\lambda}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{3}\cdot n(n-1)(n-2)\,\frac{\chi_{\lambda}((1\,2\,3))}{\chi_{\lambda}(e)}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{d}\sum_{j=1}^{\lambda_{k}}(j-k)^{2}-\binom{n}{2}

\displaystyle=-\binom{n}{2}+\sum_{k=1}^{d}\left(\frac{(\lambda_{k}-k)(\lambda_{k}-k+1)(2(\lambda_{k}-k)+1)}{6}+\frac{(k-1)k(2k-1)}{6}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{d(d-1)^{2}(d-2)}{2}-\binom{n}{2}+\sum_{k=1}^{d}\frac{(\lambda_{k}-k)(\lambda_{k}-k+1)(2(\lambda_{k}-k)+1)}{6}

using the formula after
[
Lsa08
, Theorem 4]
.
The values
\gamma_{k,\lambda}
separate irreps as follows.

Theorem 8.4
.

If

\lambda,\mu\vdash n
have at most
d
rows, then

\lambda=\mu\quad\iff\quad\gamma_{k,\lambda}=\gamma_{k,\mu}\text{ for all }k=2,\dots,d.

Proof.

Let
p_{d,k}=x_{1}^{k}+\cdots+x_{d}^{k}
denote the
k
th power-sum symmetric polynomial in
d
variables.
Let
\lambda\vdash n
have at most
d
rows, and write
\lambda_{i}=0
for
\het(\lambda)<i\leq d
.
By (
8.3
) we have

k\gamma_{k,\lambda}=n^{\downarrow k}\,\frac{\chi_{\lambda}((1\,\dots\,k))}{\chi_{\lambda}(e)},

where
n^{\downarrow k}
is the falling factorial.
By
[
VK81
, Lemma 5.1]
or
[
IO02
, Propositions 1.4, 3.3 and 3.4]
,

k\gamma_{k,\lambda}=\Big(p_{d,k}+P_{k}\big(p_{d,1},\dots,p_{d,k-1}\big)\Big)\big(\lambda_{1}-1+\tfrac{1}{2},\lambda_{2}-2+\tfrac{1}{2},\dots,\lambda_{d}-d+\tfrac{1}{2}\big)

(8.4)

for some polynomial
P_{k}
in
k-1
variables.
Also note that

p_{d,1}\big(\lambda_{1}-1+\tfrac{1}{2},\lambda_{2}-2+\tfrac{1}{2},\dots,\lambda_{d}-d+\tfrac{1}{2}\big)=n-\tfrac{d^{2}}{2}.

Now assume that
\gamma_{k,\lambda}=\gamma_{k,\mu}
for all
k=2,\dots,d
. By (
8.4
),

p_{d,k}\big(\lambda_{1}-1+\tfrac{1}{2},\lambda_{2}-2+\tfrac{1}{2},\dots,\lambda_{d}-d+\tfrac{1}{2}\big)=p_{d,k}\big(\mu_{1}-1+\tfrac{1}{2},\mu_{2}-2+\tfrac{1}{2},\dots,\mu_{d}-d+\tfrac{1}{2}\big)\qquad

for all
k=1,\dots,d
. Since

\lambda_{1}-1+\tfrac{1}{2}>\lambda_{2}-2+\tfrac{1}{2}>\cdots>\lambda_{d}-d+\tfrac{1}{2},\quad\mu_{1}-1+\tfrac{1}{2}>\mu_{2}-2+\tfrac{1}{2}>\cdots>\mu_{d}-d+\tfrac{1}{2}

and the power-sum symmetric polynomials distinguish points up to a coordinate shuffle,
it follows that
\lambda=\mu
.
∎

For
k\in\mathbb{N}
denote

c_{k}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i_{0},\dots,i_{k}\leq d\\[1.42262pt]
\text{pairwise distinct},\\[1.42262pt]
i_{0}<i_{j}\text{ for }j\geq 1\end{subarray}}\text{swap}_{i_{0}i_{1}}\text{swap}_{i_{0}i_{2}}\cdots\text{swap}_{i_{0}i_{k}}\in\mathcal{F}_{n}

(8.5)

which corresponds to
q_{k+1}\in\mathbb{C}[S_{n}]
.
By Theorem
8.4
, finding the largest eigenvalue of the localized
d
-QMC Hamiltonian
H_{G}^{\lambda}
(for
\het(\lambda)\leq d
) is equivalent to the NPO problem

\min\left\{\alpha:\alpha-h_{G}=\sum_{k}s_{k}^{*}s_{k}+q\text{ for some }s_{k}\in\mathcal{F}_{n},\ q\in\mathcal{I}^{\text{Sw}_{d}}_{n}+\big(c_{k}-\gamma_{k+1,\lambda}\colon k\leq d-1\big)\right\}.

As in Section
5
, this NPO can be solved through a hierarchy of SDP relaxations.

Author contributions

All authors contributed extensively to the development and the writing of this manuscript.

Appendix A
Linear subspace of
M_{d^{n}}(\mathbb{C})
spanned by the products of at most
d-1
swap matrices

Here we prove that in
M^{\text{Sw}_{d}}_{n}(\mathbb{C}),
there are no relations of order at most
d-1
(in the swap matrices, represented by transpositions) other than (
4.1
).

Remark A.1
.

Note that if a permutation
\sigma
is a product of disjoint cycles of lengths
\ell_{1},\dots,\ell_{k}
respectively, then
\sigma
is can be written as a product of
\sum_{i=1}^{k}(\ell_{i}-1)
transpositions (and not fewer than that many transpositions).

First we note that linear independence of swap operators is preserved if the local dimension increases.

Lemma A.2
.

If a set of products of swap operators is linearly independent in
M^{\text{{Sw}}_{d}}_{n}(\mathbb{C})
, then it is linearly independent in
M^{\text{{Sw}}_{d+1}}_{n}(\mathbb{C})
.

Proof.

By Theorem
2.2
, the algebra
M^{\text{{Sw}}_{d}}_{n}(\mathbb{C})
can be obtained as a quotient of
M^{\text{{Sw}}_{d+1}}_{n}(\mathbb{C}),
namely by modding out the direct summands
\rho_{\lambda}(\mathbb{C}S_{n}),
where
\lambda
is a partition of
n
with exactly
d
rows.
The statement then follows since any linearly independent set in a quotient
M^{\text{{Sw}}_{d+1}}_{n}(\mathbb{C})
is linearly independent in
M^{\text{{Sw}}_{d+1}}_{n}(\mathbb{C}).

∎

The following statement is the main result of this section.

Proposition A.3
.

The set of all products (that correspond to distinct permutations) of at most
d-1
swap matrices
is a basis of the subspace of
M^{\text{Sw}_{d}}_{n}(\mathbb{C})
of polynomials in the Swap
ij
of degree at most
d-1.

Remark A.4
.

In other words, Proposition
A.3
states that permutations, which are products of at most
d-1
transpositions, are linearly independent as elements of
M^{\text{Sw}_{d}}_{n}(\mathbb{C})
.
In Section
5
, we mentioned another natural linearly independent subset of
M^{\text{Sw}_{d}}_{n}(\mathbb{C})
. Recall that a permutation
\pi\in S_{n}
is called
(d+1)
-good
if there is no increasing sequence
j_{0}<\cdots<j_{d}
such that
\pi(j_{0})>\cdots>\pi_{(}j_{d})
. Then
(d+1)
-good permutations form a basis of
M^{\text{Sw}_{d}}_{n}(\mathbb{C})
by
[
Pro21
, Theorem 8]
.
However, Proposition
A.3
is not a direct consequence of this result. Namely, a product of at most
d-1
transpositions is not necessarily a
(d+1)
-good permutation if
d\geq 3
. Concretely, the product of
d-1
disjoint transpositions
\pi=\prod_{i=1}^{d-1}(i,2d-1-i)
satisfies
\pi(1)>\pi(2)>\cdots>\pi(2d-2)
, so it is not
2(d-1)
-good (and in particular, not
(d+1)
-good if
d\geq 3
).

Before proving Proposition
A.3
, we require two lemmas.

Lemma A.5
.

Let
\sigma
be a permutation in
S_{n}
that is a product of
d-1
transpositions and cannot be written as a product of fewer than
d-1
transpositions.
Let
v\in(\mathbb{C}^{d})^{\otimes n}
be an elementary tensor whose factors are standard basis vectors (so that
v
has at most
d
distinct indices). Suppose that for any product
\tau
of
k
disjoint cycles of
\sigma,
where
k=1,2,
the part of
v
on which
\tau
acts has at most
k-1
indices that are repeated and they occur at most twice. Then
\sigma
acts uniquely on
v
among the products of at most
d-1
transpositions.

Proof.

Let
\sigma
and
v
be as in Lemma
A.5
. If
\sigma
is a permutation on strictly less than
n
letters, add to its cyclic structure the singletons corresponding to the missing letters in
\{1,\ldots,n\}.

First suppose
\tau
is one of the disjoint cycles of
\sigma
and let
w
be the part of
v
on which
\tau
acts.
If
w
has an index that appears at least twice, then one can construct at least one other permutation
\tau^{\prime}
that is a product of at most as many transpositions as
\tau
, and gives the same result when applied to
w.
Indeed, to find the first cycle of
\tau^{\prime},
start with the index of
w
that is repeated, then find its image among the remaining indices, take the image of the latter and continue until the starting index occurs again. Since the starting index occurs at least twice in
w,
the produced cycle is of smaller length compared to
\sigma.
Now repeat the procedure starting with any other index to deduce what the other disjoint cycles are. This way we break the action of
\sigma
on
w
into an action of a product of disjoint cycles (some of them may have length one) whose lengths sum up to the length of
\sigma.
Hence their product can be written as a product
of strictly less than
d-1
transpositions.

Now let
\tau=\tau_{1}\tau_{2}
be a product of two disjoint cycles of
\sigma
and let
w_{1}
and
w_{2}
be the parts of
v
on which
\tau_{1}
and
\tau_{2}
act, respectively. Suppose that none of
w_{1}
and
w_{2}
has repeated indices, but they do share at least two indices. For simplicity suppose they share exactly two indices. We want to construct another permutation
\tau^{\prime}
that is a product of at most as many transpositions as
\tau
, and gives the same result when applied to
w_{1}\otimes w_{2}.
As before, for the first cycle of
\tau^{\prime}
start with one of the indices with two occurrences in
w_{1},
say
j_{1},
then find its image among the remaining indices, take the image of the latter and continue until
j_{1}
occurs again. Note that the first time when the image of a letter is the other index with two occurrences, call it
j_{2},
there are two choices: to consider the image of
j_{2}
by either
\tau_{1}
or
\tau_{2}
and then continue the process until the image of a letter is
j_{1}
again. Note that if we choose to continue with
\tau_{1}(j_{2}),
we get back
\tau,
but if we consider

\tau_{2}(j_{2}),
we switch to the other cycle and finish with the factor
e_{j_{1}}
of
w_{2}.
Hence, the second option produces the first cycle of the permutation
\tau^{\prime}
we are looking for. For the second cycle of
\tau^{\prime}
restart the procedure with the index
j_{2}
of a factor in
w_{1}.
By construction,
\tau^{\prime}
is also a product of at most
d-1
transpositions.

It remains to prove that if
\sigma
meets the conditions in Lemma
A.5
, then it acts on
v
uniquely among the products of at most
d-1
transpositions. This is true by the same procedure as above of deducing the cyclic structure by comparing
v
to its image
\sigma(v).
Indeed, start with any index, take its image and continue until the starting index occurs again.

The only time we have two options during this process is when we hit an index
j
in
v
that occurs (exactly) twice across two cycles, say
\tau_{1}
and
\tau_{2}.
Denote the parts of
v
on which
\tau_{i}
acts by
w_{i}.
Suppose we started the process with
j
in
\tau_{1}.
When we hit
j
again, we can either terminate the process (which yields the cycle
\tau_{1}
) or continue with the image of
j
by
\tau_{2}.
In this case we join the two cycles
\tau_{1}
and
\tau_{2},
which means that the resulting permutation must have at least one transposition more than
\sigma.

If we start with an index
k\neq j
of
\tau_{1},
then, when we first hit
j,
there are again two options: either to continue with
\tau_{1}(j)
or
\tau_{2}(j).
The choice
\tau_{1}(j)
at the end reproduces
\tau_{1}
(actually, it may happen that the index
k
occurs in another cycle, say
\tau_{3},
and we may switch to
\tau_{3}
after hitting
k
again, but this case was already treated before). With the choice
\tau_{2}(j)
we switch to the other cycle
\tau_{2}
and since
k
does not occur in
w_{2},
the process does not terminate in
w_{2}
(meaning that we need to switch cycle once more before terminating). This again means that we join (at least) two cycles and the resulting permutation must have at least one transposition more than
\sigma.

This shows that
\sigma
is the only permutation that can be written as a product of at most
d-1
transpositions and gives the result
\sigma(v)
when applied to
v.

∎

Example A.6
.

Let
d=4,n=5
and define
\sigma=(12)(345).
Let

v_{1}=e_{1}\otimes e_{2}\otimes e_{1}\otimes e_{2}\otimes e_{3}\quad\text{ and }\quad v_{2}=e_{1}\otimes e_{2}\otimes e_{1}\otimes e_{3}\otimes e_{4}.

It is easy to see that since
\sigma
has two disjoint cycles and
v_{1}
has two indices that occur twice,
\sigma
acts on
v_{1}
in the same way as
\sigma^{\prime}=(14)(253).
On the other hand, by the algorithm of deducing the cyclic structure by comparing a vector to its image,
\sigma
acts on
v_{2}
uniquely among the products of at most
3
transpositions in
S_{5}.

Lemma A.7
.

Let
\sigma
in
S_{n}
be a product of
d-1
transpositions that cannot be written as a product of fewer than
d-1
transpositions. Then there is a vector
v\in(\mathbb{C}^{d})^{\otimes n}
whose tensor factors are standard basis vectors with at most
d
distinct indices that meets the conditions in Lemma
A.5
.

Proof.

Let
\sigma\in S_{n}
be a product of
d-1
transpositions which cannot be written as a product of less than
d-1
transpositions. Suppose
\sigma
is a product of
k
disjoint cycles for some
k=1,\ldots,d-1
(all the cycles being of length
2
or more). Hence,
\sigma
is a permutation on
d+k-1
letters, but the factors of
v
are chosen among the
d
standard basis vectors of
\mathbb{C}^{d}.

Without loss of generality assume that the letters of
\sigma
are
1,\ldots,d+k-1.
Order the cycles of
\sigma
increasingly by their lengths.
Then assign the indices
i_{j}
to the first
d+k-1
factors
e_{i_{j}}
of
v
in the following way:

Assign indices
1,\ldots,d
(e.g., increasingly according to the position of the factors) to the part of
v
on which the cycles of
\sigma
involving letters
1,\ldots,d
act (the cycle with
d
may involve larger indices and hence we do not yet assign the indices to all of the factors of
v
on which this cycle acts). Now
v
has
k-1
more factors to be labeled (with indices between
1
and
d
), hence the corresponding part of
\sigma
has at most
\lfloor(k-1)/2\rfloor
disjoint cycles. Since we have
k
cycles in total (each of length at least
2
), the part of
\sigma
on the letters
1,\ldots,d
is a product of at least
\lfloor(k+1)/2\rfloor
disjoint cycles.

So assign to the next
\lfloor(k-1)/2\rfloor
unlabeled factors of
v
(e.g., increasingly according to the position of the factors) the first letters of the cycles of
\sigma
on the letters
1,\ldots,d.
Finally, assign to the remaining unlabeled factors of
v
the second letters of the cycles of
\sigma
on the letters
1,\ldots,d.

This way we labeled the first
d+k-1
factors of
v.
To the remaining factors just assign the index
1.

It is now clear from the construction that the obtained vector meets the conditions in Lemma
A.5
.
∎

Example A.8
.

Let us illustrate Lemmas
A.5
and
A.7
in the case
n=8
and
d=5.

Suppose
\sigma\in S_{8}
is a product of
4
transpositions and it cannot be written as a product of less than
4
transpositions (here we omit writing the singletons in
\sigma
). Then we have
4
options:

1.

If
\sigma
is a
5
-cycle, e.g.,
\sigma=(12345),
then a suitable vector is

v=e_{1}\otimes e_{2}\otimes e_{3}\otimes e_{4}\otimes e_{5}\otimes e_{1}\otimes e_{1}\otimes e_{1}.

2.

If
\sigma
has two cycles, there are two possible cyclic structures: two
3
-cycles or a product of a transposition and a
4
-cycle. E.g.,
\sigma=(123)(456)
or
\sigma=(12)(3456).
In both cases we can take

v=e_{1}\otimes e_{2}\otimes e_{3}\otimes e_{4}\otimes e_{5}\otimes e_{1}\otimes e_{1}\otimes e_{1}.

3.

If
\sigma
has
3
cycles, then it must be a product of two transpositions and a
3
-cycle. E.g., if
\sigma=(12)(34)(567),
we can take

v=e_{1}\otimes e_{2}\otimes e_{3}\otimes e_{4}\otimes e_{5}\otimes e_{1}\otimes e_{3}\otimes e_{1}.

4.

If
\sigma
has
4
cycles, then it must be a product of four transpositions. If, e.g.,
\sigma=(12)(34)(56)(78),
we can take

v=e_{1}\otimes e_{2}\otimes e_{3}\otimes e_{4}\otimes e_{5}\otimes e_{1}\otimes e_{2}\otimes e_{4}.

Proof of Proposition
A.3
.

Denote the set of all products (that correspond to distinct permutations) of at most
d-1
swap matrices by
\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_{d-1}.
Suppose

\sum_{s\in\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_{d-1}}\alpha_{s}s=0

for some scalars
\alpha_{s}
. By Lemma
A.5
and Lemma
A.7
, for each product
s
of
d-1
transpositions that cannot be written as a product of less than
d-1
transpositions there is an elementary tensor vector
v_{s}\in(\mathbb{C}^{d})^{\otimes n}
such that
s
acts uniquely on
v_{s}
among the elements of
\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_{d-1}.

Since elements of
\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_{d-1}
act on
(\mathbb{C}^{d})^{\otimes n}
as permutations of tensor factors, this means that
s\cdot v_{s}
is linearly independent of
\{t\cdot v_{s}\colon t\in\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_{d-1}\setminus\{s\}\}
.
Hence,
\alpha_{s}=0
for all
s\in\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_{d-1}
that cannot be written as products of less than
d-1
transpositions. Now use induction and Lemma
A.2
to finish the proof.
∎

Appendix B
Proof of Proposition
7.8

Recall that for any partition
\zeta=(\zeta_{1},\dots,\zeta_{d})
of an integer
m
, we define the function
\eta(\zeta)
as:

\eta(\zeta)=m^{2}+\frac{d(d-1)(2d-1)}{6}-\sum_{i=1}^{d}(\zeta_{i}-(i-1))^{2}.

Our first goal in this section is to derive a formula for

\Delta(\lambda,\mu,\nu)=\eta(\lambda)-\eta(\mu)-\eta(\nu)

(B.1)

in terms of the contents of the boxes of the skew-shaped Young diagrams associated with these partitions. The
content of a box
at row
r
and column
c
in a skew-shaped Young diagram
\zeta
is defined as
\text{content}(\text{box})=c-r
(not to be confused with the content of a Young tableau as in Section
7.2.1
). Let
\Sigma(\zeta)
denote the sum of contents of boxes in the skew-shaped Young diagram
\zeta
(or the Young diagram of
\zeta
, if the latter is a partition).

Lemma B.1
.

Let
\lambda\vdash n
,
\mu\vdash n-k
and
\nu\vdash k
. If
\mu
is contained in
\lambda
, then

\Delta(\lambda,\mu,\nu)=2k(n-k)-2\Sigma(\lambda/\mu)+2\Sigma(\nu).

Proof.

By
[
Lsa08
, Theorem 4]
or
[
Fro00
]
, contents of boxes in a Young diagram of
\zeta\vdash m
are related to the character of
\zeta
as
\frac{\chi_{\zeta}((ij))}{\chi_{\zeta}(e)}=\binom{n}{2}^{-1}\Sigma(\zeta)
. By Lemma
6.5
,

\eta(\zeta)=m^{2}-m-2\Sigma(\zeta).

(B.2)

We replace
\eta(\lambda),\eta(\mu),\eta(\nu)
in
\Delta(\lambda,\mu,\nu)
with (
B.2
),
and note that
\Sigma(\lambda/\mu)=\Sigma(\lambda)-\Sigma(\mu)
since
\mu
is contained in
\lambda
.
∎

The rest of this appendix consists of the proof of Proposition
7.8
(stated below for convenience) divided in several cases.

Proposition B.2
.

When
k\leq 4
or
d\leq 3
, the expression (
7.5
) is maximized at a triple of partitions
\lambda\vdash n
,
\mu\vdash n-k
and
\nu\vdash k
such that
\lambda=\mu\uplus\nu
.
For such partitions, the coefficient
c^{\lambda}_{\mu\nu}
is nonzero.

We shall also paraphrase
\lambda=\mu\uplus\nu
by saying that each row of
\lambda
coincides either with a row of
\mu
or a row of
\nu
.
For such a triple
(\lambda,\mu,\nu)
, we first check that
c^{\lambda}_{\mu\nu}\neq 0
.
Since
c^{\lambda}_{\mu\nu}=c^{\lambda}_{\nu\mu}
, we can without loss of generality assume that the first row of
\lambda
equals the first row of
\mu
.
Then
c^{\lambda}_{\mu\nu}=c^{\lambda^{\prime}}_{\mu^{\prime}\nu}
, where
\lambda^{\prime},\mu^{\prime}
are obtained from
\lambda,\mu
by deleting the first row. In the triple
(\lambda^{\prime},\mu^{\prime},\nu)
, each row of
\lambda^{\prime}
coincides either with a row of
\mu^{\prime}
or a row of
\nu
. Thus, we can continue inductively until one of
\mu
or
\nu
is the empty partition, and the other one equals
\lambda
(in which case the corresponding Littlewood-Richardson coefficient is nonzero).

Such triples in particular arise as follows. Let
\mu
be contained in
\lambda
. If
\mu
and
\lambda/\mu
do not share any rows, then
\mu
is a subpartition of
\lambda
and
\lambda/\mu
is a partition, and
\lambda=\mu\uplus\lambda/\mu=(\mu,\lambda/\mu)
. The relation “
\mu
and
\lambda/\mu
do not share any rows” will thus frequently appear in the proof of Proposition
B.2
below.

The proof of the first (main) statement of Proposition
B.2
relies on particular joint rearrangements of partitions
\lambda,\mu,\nu
that arise from moving a single box.
Assume
c_{\mu\nu}^{\lambda}\neq 0
(so in particular,
\mu
is contained in
\lambda
).
That is, one can label the skew-shaped diagram
\lambda/\mu
as an LR tableau with content
\nu
.
A move of a box of
\lambda/\mu
is called a
Robin Hood move
if the change in the box’s content is non-decreasing and, after the move, there exists an LR tableau on the obtained skew-shaped diagram
\lambda^{\prime}/\mu^{\prime}
with content
\nu^{\prime}
whose height is not larger than the height of
\nu
.
In particular, a Robin Hood move returns a triple
(\lambda^{\prime},\mu^{\prime},\nu^{\prime})
with
c^{\lambda^{\prime}}_{\mu^{\prime}\nu^{\prime}}\neq 0
, and does not decrease the
\Delta
value by Lemma
B.1
:

\Delta(\lambda,\mu,\nu)\leq\Delta(\lambda^{\prime},\mu^{\prime},\nu^{\prime})
.
Furthermore, it suffices to only consider moving the boxes of
\lambda/\mu
with minimal contents (as these minimize the increment of
\Sigma(\lambda/\mu)
).

B.1
Case
k=2

The partitions of
2
satisfy
\eta_{(2)}<\eta_{(1,1)}
.
To prove Proposition
B.2
we separate three cases, based on
d-e\in\{0,1,2\}
.

B.1.1
Case
d-e=2

Here,
\lambda_{d-1},\lambda_{d}
equal 1 and form
\nu=(1,1)
. Hence,
\mu
and
\lambda/\mu
do not share rows.

B.1.2
Case
d-e=1

(a) If
e=d-1
and
\lambda_{d}=2,
then
\lambda
and
\lambda/\mu
do not share rows.

(b-1) If
\lambda_{d}=1
and
\mu_{d-1}=2,
then there is one
\lambda/\mu
box in row
d.
Moving the other
\lambda/\mu
box next to it (is a Robin Hood move and hence) yields a higher
\Delta
value.

(b-2) If
\lambda_{d}=1
and
\mu_{d-1}=1,
let
j_{0}
be the smallest row index
j
such that
\mu_{j}=1.
Then move the
\lambda/\mu
box that is not in row
d
to row
j_{0}.
This is a Robin Hood move by definition of
j_{0},
hence
\Delta
increases. By exchanging
\lambda_{d-1}
with
\mu_{j_{0}},
we produce a triple of partitions with the same
\Delta
value such that
\lambda/\mu
and
\mu
do not share rows.

B.1.3
Case
d=e

(a) If
\mu_{d-1}\geq 2,
then move the the
\mu
boxes from row
d
to the first row and place the two
\lambda/\mu
boxes in row
d
(Robin Hood moves). This clearly increases the value of
\Delta.

(b) If
\mu_{d-1}=1,
then
\mu_{d}=1.
Move both
\mu_{d-1}
and
\mu_{d}
to the first row and place the
\lambda/\mu
boxes in row
d-1
and
d,
respectively.
Now the contents of these two boxes are lowered, but the height of
\nu
may increase. We shall prove that the value of
\Delta
increases in any case.

Assume
\nu=(2)
at the start. So, after the moves,
\Sigma(\nu)
decreases by
1.

Denote the two
\lambda/\mu
boxes by b1 and b.
Since the initial content of b1 is
\geq 2-(d-2)=4-d,

\Sigma(\lambda/\mu)
increases by
>1
after the move, causing
the increase in the value of
\Delta.

After the move,
\nu
is the tail of
\lambda,
so
\lambda=\mu\uplus\nu.

B.2
Case
k=3

The partitions of 3 satisfy
\eta_{(3)}<\eta_{(2,1)}<\eta_{(1,1,1)}
. The proof of Proposition
B.2
again splits in several cases.

B.2.1
Case
d-e=3

The tail of
\lambda
equals
\nu=(1,1,1).
In this case
\mu
and
\lambda/\mu
do not share any rows and we are done.

B.2.2
Case
d-e=2

The three boxes of
\lambda/\mu
are not stacked on top of each other. So a minimal enumeration of
\lambda/\mu
does not include
3
.
Since the goal is to maximize
\Delta
as in (
B.1
), we can assume
\nu\neq(1,1,1)
(if we can increase the value of
\Delta(\lambda,\mu,(2,1)),
we can also increase the value of
\Delta(\lambda,\mu,(1,1,1))
).
In this case, the minimal enumeration of
\lambda/\mu
includes
2
, so we can assume
\nu=(2,1).

(a) If the tail of
\lambda
equals
\nu,
i.e.,
\lambda_{d-1}=2,\lambda_{d}=1,
then
\lambda
and
\lambda/\mu
do not share any rows and we are done.

(b) If
\nu
is not the tail of
\lambda,
then two
\lambda/\mu
boxes are in the
(d-1)
st and
d
th row, respectively, and the third is either in row
d-1
(in which case
\lambda=\mu\uplus\nu
and we are done) or at the end of some
\mu
-row. Assume the latter.

(b-1) If
\mu_{2}\geq 2,
then the third box is moved to the
(d-1)
st row. In this case
\mu
and
\nu
stay the same, but
\lambda
is changed via a Robin Hood move. Hence the value of
\Delta
increases. After the move,
\lambda=\mu\uplus\nu
and we are done.

(b-2) If
\mu_{e}=1,
let
j
be the largest row index such that
\mu_{j}>1.
Move the third box of
\lambda/\mu
at the end of row
\mu_{j+1}.
This move is clearly a Robin Hood move by definition of
j.
Since
\mu
and
\nu
do not change, the value of
\Delta
increases. Now by exchanging
\lambda_{d-1}
with
\mu_{j+1},
we see that each row of
\lambda
is either a row of
\mu
or a row of
\nu,
i.e.,
\lambda=\mu\uplus\nu.

B.2.3
Case
d-e=1

(a) If
\mu_{d-1}\geq 3,
then move the
2
boxes (that are potentially not yet in the
d
th row) of
\lambda/\mu
to the
d
th row. Since this process only involves Robin Hood moves and
\mu
does not change, the value of
\Delta
increases. After this move, clearly,
\lambda=\mu\uplus\nu.

(b) If
\mu_{d-1}=2,
then move to the
d
th row one of the two boxes of
\lambda/\mu
that are not in the
d
th row (this is a Robin Hood move, hence
\Delta
increases).

Similarly as in (b-2) in the previous subsection, let
j
be the largest row index such that
\mu_{j}>2.
Move the third box of
\lambda/\mu
at the end of row
\mu_{j+1}.
This move is again a Robin Hood move by definition of
j.
Since
\mu
and
\nu
do not change, the value of
\Delta
increases. By exchanging
\lambda_{d-1}
with
\mu_{j+1},
we see that each row of
\lambda
is either a row of
\mu
or a row of
\nu.
Thus,
\lambda=\mu\uplus\nu.

(c) Now assume
\mu_{d-1}=1.

(c-1.1) If
\lambda_{d-1}=\mu_{d-1}=1
and
\mu_{d-2}>1,
then exchange the
\mu
box in row
d-1
with one
\lambda/\mu
box in row
d-2.
To compute tha change in the value of
\Delta
assume the worst case where
\nu=(3).
After the exchange,
\Sigma(\nu)
decreases by
3
and
\Sigma(\lambda/\mu)
increases by
2.

Now move the remaining
\lambda/\mu
box that is not in row
d-1
or row
d
to the
(d-1)
st row. This is clearly a Robin Hood move, so the value of
\Delta
increases by at least
2
after this move. The value of
\Delta
hence increases after these two moves and at the end,
\nu
is the tail of
\lambda,
i.e.,
\lambda=\mu\uplus\nu.

(c-1.2) If
\lambda_{d-1}=\mu_{d-1}=\mu_{d-2}=\lambda_{d-2}=1,
then move the
\mu
boxes from the
(d-1)
st and
(d-2)
nd row to the first and the second row, respectively and move the two
\lambda/\mu
boxes that are not in the
d
th row to the
(d-1)
st and
(d-2)
nd row, respectively.
Again, assume the worst case, where
\nu=(3)
at the start.
Note that after the move,
\nu=(1,1,1).
So, after the move,
\Sigma(\nu)
decreases by
6.

However, by assumption, the contents of the two
\lambda/\mu
boxes that are not in the first column (call them b2 and b3), satisfy content(b2)
\geq 2-d+3
and content(b3)
\geq 3-d+3.

Hence,
\Sigma(\lambda/\mu)
increases by at least
6
after the move and hence the value of
\Delta
does not decrease.
Moreover, after the move,
\nu
is the tail of
\lambda,
so
\lambda=\mu\uplus\nu.

(c-1.3) If
\lambda_{d-1}=\mu_{d-1}=\mu_{d-2}=1,
but
\lambda_{d-2}>1,
then first exchange the
\mu
box in row
d-1
with a
\lambda/\mu
box in row
d-2.

Again, assume
\nu=(3)
at the beginning. Note that after this move,
\nu=(2,1),
so
\Sigma(\nu)
decreases by
3
and
\Sigma(\lambda/\mu)
increases by
2.

Next, move the remaining
\lambda/\mu
box (the one not in the
1
st column) to row
d-1.
This move increases the value of
\Delta
by at least
1,
so the value of
\Delta
in fact increases after these two moves. Moreover,
\nu
becomes the tail of
\lambda,
so
\lambda=\mu\uplus\nu.

(c-2) Assume
\mu_{d-1}=1
and
\lambda_{d-1}=2.

(c-2.1) If
\lambda_{d}=2,
move the
\mu
box from row
d-1
to the first row and rearrange the
\nu
boxes so that
\lambda_{d-1}=2
and
\lambda_{d}=1.

It is easy to compute that the value of
\Delta
does not change after this move.
Moreover,
\nu
becomes the tail of
\lambda,
so
\lambda=\mu\uplus\nu
and we are done.

(c-2.2) If
\mu_{d-1}=1,\lambda_{d-1}=2
and
\lambda_{d}=1,
then move one
\lambda/\mu
box to row
d
(which is a Robin Hood move) to end up with case (c-2.1).

B.2.4
Case
d=e

(a) If
\mu_{d-1}\geq 3,
then move the
\mu
boxes in row
d
to the first row and move the
\lambda/\mu
boxes to row
d.
these are Robin Hood moves, hence the value of
\Delta
increases. Now
\nu
is the tail of
\lambda,
so
\lambda=\mu\uplus\nu.

(b) If
\mu_{d-1}=2,
then move the
\mu
boxes in rows
d-1
and
d
to the first row and place the
\lambda/\mu
boxes in rows
d-1
and
d
so that
\lambda_{d-1}=2
and
\lambda_{d}=1.
Doing so, the contents of these three boxes decrease, but the height of
\nu
might increase.

So assume that
\nu=(3)
at the start. Then, after the move,

\Sigma(\nu)
decreases by
3.

By assumption (
\mu_{d-1}=2
), each of the three boxes in
\lambda/\mu
satisfies

\text{content(box)}>3-d
.
Hence,
\Sigma(\lambda/\mu)
increases by
>3
and the value of
\Delta
increases,
After this moves,
\nu
is the tail of
\lambda,
so
\lambda=\mu\uplus\nu.

(c) If
\mu_{d-1}=1,
then move
\mu_{d},\mu_{d-1}
and
\mu_{d-2}
to the first row and move the three
\lambda/\mu
boxes to rows
d,d-1,d-2,
respectively, so that
\lambda_{d}=\lambda_{d-1}=\lambda_{d-2}=1.
Note that
